### NGINX HTTP/2 Use-After-Free: CVE-2026-123633
- Source: Palo Alto Unit 42 | Published: 2026-11-24T23:00:14Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-123633, CVE-2026-123634 | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: Technical analysis of CVE-2026-123633, an NGINX HTTP/2 use-after-free flaw, with details on affected versions, sanitizer tests, and patch guidance. The post NGINX HTTP/2 Use-After-Free: CVE-2026-123633 appeared first on Unit 42 .
- Technical details: Technical analysis of CVE-2026-123633, an NGINX HTTP/2 use-after-free flaw, with details on affected versions, sanitizer tests, and patch guidance.

CVE-2026-123633 is a use-after-free vulnerability in NGINX HTTP/2 that may let an unauthenticated attacker cause a denial of service. In the CVE record, F5 says code execution may be possible when address space layout randomization (ASLR) is disabled or bypassed.

CVE-2026-123633 affects NGINX servers that accept HTTP/2 client connections and proxy requests to a backend when free worker connections run low. A fragmented request can leave an active connection marked reusable. Under connection pressure, NGINX can close that connection and free memory the active request still uses.

F5's CVE record scores the issue 9.2 Critical under CVSS 4.0. The NGINX security advisories page labels the issue medium and lists versions 1.19.7 through 1.30.6 and 1.31.0 through 1.31.8 as affected. F5 credits memN0ps (Palo Alto Networks Unit 42) among the reporters.

NGINX 1.30.7 on the stable branch and 1.31.9 on the mainline branch contain the fix. F5 also lists affected NGINX Plus releases .

Administrators should review HTTP/2 reverse-proxy exposure and upgrade to a fixed NGINX release or a vendor package containing the CVE-2026-123633 fix.

Palo Alto Networks customers are better protected from the threats discussed above through the following products and services:

Organizations can access tailored adversary intelligence, direct analyst consultations, and specialized exposure tracking through Unit 42 Threat Intel Services and its Deep and Dark Web Service .

If you think you might have been compromised or have an urgent matter, contact the Unit 42 Incident Response team .

While researching NGINX, I independently found the HTTP/2 connection-state defect now tracked as CVE-2026-123633.

An unauthenticated client can send a fragmented HTTP/2 request that leaves an active connection marked as reusable. When the server resolves a hostname during the request and free connection slots run low, NGINX can reclaim that connection mid-request and free memory its DNS resolver is still using.

I reproduced the use-after-free with AddressSanitizer (ASan) on builds of unmodified NGINX 1.31.7 and 1.31.8 source. The NGINX security advisories page lists 1.19.7 through 1.30.6 and 1.31.0 through 1.31.8 as affected and 1.30.7 and 1.31.9 as fixed releases.

I reported the HTTP/2 use-after-free privately to F5. F5's CVE record scores it 9.2 Critical under CVSS 4.0 and 8.1 High under CVSS 3.1. The NGINX security advisories page labels it Medium.

In my ASan test on a Debian guest, ASan stopped NGINX at the first read from freed DNS lookup memory.

The resolver path in my test stems from the HTTP/2 connection bug tracked as CVE-2026-123633. CVE-2026-123634 is a separate DNS resolver vulnerability fixed in the same release.

I also found a memory disclosure vulnerability that leaked a libc pointer. I used the address to calculate the libc base and work around ASLR when chaining the disclosure with CVE-2026-123633. I reported this additional bug to F5 as a separate issue. From an attacker VM, I achieved pre-authentication remote code execution on a target VM as the non-root NGINX worker. The chain required a non-default NGINX configuration, and some attempts did not open a shell.

F5 assessed the memory disclosure as a behavior limited to a non-typical configuration and has not assigned it a CVE at the time of writing, so I am leaving out its details and the exploit steps.

NGINX marks a connection reusable when it can close that connection under pressure. Stream creation should clear the reusable mark once a request starts, but ngx_http_v2_create_stream() leaves it set in affected releases.

When a client sends only part of an HTTP/2 HEADERS frame header, ngx_http_v2_handle_connection() marks the connection reusable and returns early. The code below shows that decision:
- Fix / remediation: Administrators should review HTTP/2 reverse-proxy exposure and upgrade to a fixed NGINX release or a vendor package containing the CVE-2026-123633 fix. CVE-2026-123634 is a separate DNS resolver vulnerability fixed in the same release.

### Rackspace shuts down Spot cloud platform after security breach
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-24T21:37:30Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: cloud
- Summary: Rackspace is shutting down its Rackspace Spot cloud platform after a security breach allowed hackers to access customer Kubernetes clusters and secrets, giving customers until December 8 to migrate their services and download their data. [...]
- Technical details: Rackspace is shutting down its Rackspace Spot cloud platform after a security breach allowed hackers to access customer Kubernetes clusters and secrets, giving customers until December 8 to migrate their services and download their data.

Rackspace Spot is a cloud platform that allows organizations to deploy and manage containerized applications using Kubernetes.

The company announced the shutdown in a new security notification sent to customers and obtained by BleepingComputer, saying it had decided to discontinue the service after reviewing the incident.

"Following a review of the situation, Rackspace has made the decision to wind down the Rackspace Spot service. This decision is being made to provide a clear path forward for customers," reads the notification.

Rackspace says the service will be permanently discontinued on December 8, 2026, at 5 PM Central Time, when all remaining workloads will be terminated, and customer data will no longer be accessible.

Customers have been instructed to retrieve their data and migrate their workloads to different platforms before the deadline.

The decision comes after hackers breached Rackspace Spot on November 17 using compromised credentials, allowing them to create unauthorized pods in customer Kubernetes clusters, read Kubernetes Secrets, and list nodes and volumes.

Rackspace discovered the intrusion on November 18 following a customer report and disabled access to the platform's administration console while securing its infrastructure. Service was restored on November 21 following an extended outage.

"An unauthorized party gained access to parts of the Spot platform and may have read cluster Secrets and files on storage volumes," Rackspace said.

The company says the affected Spot environment is segmented from its core infrastructure and that it has not identified evidence of unauthorized access to its core systems or the environments of other Rackspace customers.

Rackspace is now instructing all Spot customers to treat their API tokens, refresh tokens, kubeconfigs, and Kubernetes Secrets as potentially compromised, regardless of whether they previously received a breach notification.

Customers have also been advised to review access logs dating back to November 16 and search for suspicious Kubernetes objects associated with the attack, including:

Suspicious Kubernetes pod and object prefixes: i1 , glm- , and probe

The shutdown announcement has prompted disappointment among Rackspace Spot customers, with some urging the company to reconsider and offering to pay more to keep the platform operational.

In a Reddit discussion , one customer said they believed users would be willing to pay higher prices to keep the service running.

Other customers expressed similar concerns in a separate discussion , with some asking about affordable alternatives and others describing the impact the shutdown would have on their existing infrastructure.

Others praised the service for its affordability, saying alternative cloud providers would be more expensive.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text

On October 21, 2026, Node.js announced the availability of fixes for multiple vulnerabilities across the following supported release lines: 22.x, 24.x, and 26.x.

These vulnerabilities impact critical operations such as the Brotli decoder, DNS resolution, and TLS certificate validation. The vulnerabilities pose risks for remote code execution and traffic interception. For more information, see Node.js Security Release .

The following vulnerabilities have a Security Impact Rating (SIR) of High and are addressed in this advisory:

CVE-2026-111497 - Brotli decoder out-of-bounds write; potential remote code execution

CVE-2026-105482 - DNS resolution truncation involving embedded NUL characters

CVE-2026-105483 - TLS hostname validation bypass using fullwidth IP digits

CVE-2026-106069 - TLS hostname validation bypass with noncanonical IP addresses

For information about Splunk products and offerings, including AppDynamics, see Vulnerabilities in Node.js (CVE-2026-111497, CVE-2026-105482, CVE-2026-105483, CVE-2026-106069) on Splunk Products - October 2026

This investigation was expanded to cover Brotli outside of Node.js on October 30, 2026. The Brotli decoder flaw (CVE-2026-111497), which is in the Google Brotli C decoder, is often bundled with or linked to other software, so products beyond Node.js may be affected. For software that may be affected, see Broccoli2Shell .

Important: If a product is not listed within this advisory, Cisco's review indicates that the product is not affected by these vulnerabilities.

- CVEs: CVE-2026-104715 | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: An update for cifs-utils is now available for Red Hat Enterprise Linux 9. Red Hat Product Security has rated this update as having a security impact of Important. A Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) base score, which gives a detailed severity rating, is available for each vulnerability from the CVE link(s) in the References section.
- Technical details: An update for cifs-utils is now available for Red Hat Enterprise Linux 9.

Red Hat Product Security has rated this update as having a security impact of Important. A Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) base score, which gives a detailed severity rating, is available for each vulnerability from the CVE link(s) in the References section.

The SMB/CIFS protocol is a standard file sharing protocol widely deployed on Microsoft Windows machines. The cifs-utils packages contain tools for mounting shares on Linux using the SMB/CIFS protocol. The tools in this package work in conjunction with support in the kernel to allow one to mount a SMB/CIFS share onto a client and use it as if it were a standard Linux file system.

cifs-utils: mount.cifs credential-file option injection allows local privilege boundary bypass (CVE-2026-104715)

For more details about the security issue(s), including the impact, a CVSS score, acknowledgments, and other related information, refer to the CVE page(s) listed in the References section.

For details on how to apply this update, which includes the changes described in this advisory, refer to:

### New Inception extortion gang launches large-scale vishing campaign
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-24T20:21:20Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: ransomware, apt_intrusion, identity
- Summary: A newly formed data extortion group named 'Inception' has been linked to a large-scale campaign that targets single sign-on (SSO) credentials of multiple global brands and firms. [...]
- Technical details: A newly formed data extortion group named 'Inception' has been linked to a large-scale campaign that targets single sign-on (SSO) credentials of multiple global brands and firms.

A newly formed data extortion group named ‘Inception’ has been linked to a large-scale campaign that targets single sign-on (SSO) credentials of multiple global brands and firms.

The gang surfaced on November 17, claiming seven victims on its extortion site, including chip maker AMD, PepsiCo, Cigna Healthcare, AutoZone, and Lucid Motors, but none of them have publicly confirmed a data breach.

However, cybersecurity company ReversingLabs has been tracking Inception gang's activity and says that it has been operating since late October.

The security firm has confirmed the existence of 21 domains used for SSO phishing, 41 domains believed to be used in campaign infrastructure, and 4 other domains with suspected use.

The researchers identified an extensive phishing infrastructure impersonating dozens of major corporations, including Dropbox, Duolingo, Western Union, Indeed, Activision Blizzard, Riot Games, Twitch, Reddit, Nestlé, Anthropic, Nintendo, Starbucks, McDonald's, Paramount, and NBCUniversal.

The existence of these domains is not proof that those firms have been breached, or even targeted yet, and none have recently disclosed cyberattacks or other related incidents.

The campaign appears to rely on phone-based social engineering rather than email phishing, strongly resembling the tactics used by the infamous ShinyHunters extortion group.

Inception is using legal-themed websites that imitate corporate authentication services, primarily Okta but also NetIQ/OpenText Access Manager.

One of those authentication providers, Okta, published a report yesterday linking Inception to O-UNC-053, a threat cluster it previously associated with ShinyHunters, which has been active since March.

According to Okta, after law enforcement disrupted the ShinyHunters extortion group following the arrest of suspected members [ 1 , 2 ], and the shutdown of the leak site, O-UNC-053 continued to register new phishing domains.

In late October, the activity cluster launched its legal-themed campaign, and in mid-November, the Inception leak site went live, announcing victims that matched previous targeting.

“This gap in publishing on any leak site followed by publishing on a new data leak site with a new brand is consistent with prior shifts from Com-affiliated actors,” describes Okta .

“Before and after the change in DLS publication, O-UNC-053 maintained consistent infrastructure and TTPs, leading us to assess that the cluster has very likely changed their brand affiliation.”

Inception was quick to respond and dispute the alleged links to other threat actors in an announcement on their extortion page.

The threat actors claimed to operate independently and with no affiliation to any previous group, arguing that overlapping targets and the use of common domain registration and CDN services did not establish operational links.

They also acknowledged that private tooling is circulating among independent threat actors, and using it does not prove affiliation.

To defend against the latest Inception campaign, ReversingLabs recommends enforcing phishing-resistant authentication, including FIDO2 security keys and passkeys, and strengthening help desk identity verification procedures.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text
- EPSS context: EPSS unavailable / not applicable — FIRST.org; not an asset-specific risk score

### CVE-2026-135614 - Sensitive data redaction bypass in the troubleshooting tool in Amazon ECS MCP Server
- Source: AWS Security Bulletins | Published: 2026-11-24T19:27:19Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-135614 | Topic signals: identity, cloud, ai_threat
- Summary: Bulletin ID: 2026-200-AWS Publication Date: 11/24/2026 11:00 AM PST Description: Amazon ECS MCP Server is an open-source Model Context Protocol server that lets AI agents and assistants manage and troubleshoot Amazon ECS workloads using the operator's AWS credentials. We identified CVE-2026-135614, an issue in the troubleshooting tool of the Amazon ECS MCP Server (awslabs.ecs-mcp-server) before 0.1.36. With the sensitive data setting at its default, the troubleshooting tool returned container environment variable values and secret references from ECS task definitions without the redaction the setting is intended to apply. This might allow a remote actor who can influence the agent's input, for example through prompt injection, to obtain those values. Impacted versions: < 0.1.36 Refer to the article for the most up-to-date and complete information.
- Technical details: Bulletin ID: 2026-200-AWS Scope: AWS Content Type: Important (requires attention) Publication Date: 11/24/2026 11:00 AM PST

Amazon ECS MCP Server is an open-source Model Context Protocol server that lets AI agents and assistants manage and troubleshoot Amazon ECS workloads using the operator's AWS credentials.

We identified CVE-2026-135614 , an issue in the troubleshooting tool of the Amazon ECS MCP Server (awslabs.ecs-mcp-server) before 0.1.36. With the sensitive data setting at its default, the troubleshooting tool returned container environment variable values and secret references from ECS task definitions without the redaction the setting is intended to apply. This might allow a remote actor who can influence the agent's input, for example through prompt injection, to obtain those values.

This issue has been addressed in awslabs.ecs-mcp-server version 0.1.36 . We recommend upgrading to the latest version and ensuring any forked or derivative code is patched to incorporate the new fixes. Users should also rotate any credentials stored as plaintext task definition environment variables.

We would like to thank Liu-tong for collaborating on this issue through the coordinated vulnerability disclosure process.

Please email aws-security@amazon.com with any s ecurity questions or concerns.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text

### CVE-2026-135414 - Server-side request forgery in snapshot repository registration in Amazon OpenSearch Service
- Source: AWS Security Bulletins | Published: 2026-11-24T19:27:19Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-135414 | Topic signals: cloud
- Summary: Bulletin ID: 2026-201-AWS Publication Date: 11/24/2026 11:15 AM PST Description: Amazon OpenSearch Service is a managed service for deploying, operating, and scaling OpenSearch clusters in the AWS Cloud. We identified CVE-2026-135414, an issue in snapshot repository registration in Amazon OpenSearch Service. A user with snapshot management permissions on a domain could set a repository endpoint that caused the domain's service-operated nodes to send requests to network destinations that the user specified. This issue is addressed in service software R20260720. We recommend that customers update their domains to R20260720 or later. Impacted versions: Amazon OpenSearch Service service software before R20260720 Refer to the article for the most up-to-date and complete information.
- Technical details: Bulletin ID: 2026-201-AWS Scope: AWS Content Type: Important (requires attention) Publication Date: 11/24/2026 11:15 AM PST

Amazon OpenSearch Service is a managed service for deploying, operating, and scaling OpenSearch clusters in the AWS Cloud.

We identified CVE-2026-135414 , an issue in snapshot repository registration in Amazon OpenSearch Service. A user with snapshot management permissions on a domain could set a repository endpoint that caused the domain's service-operated nodes to send requests to network destinations that the user specified. This issue is addressed in service software R20260720. We recommend that customers update their domains to R20260720 or later.

Amazon OpenSearch Service service software before R20260720

This issue has been addressed in Amazon OpenSearch Service service software R20260720. We recommend that customers update their domains to service software R20260720 or later. For instructions, see Service software updates in Amazon OpenSearch Service .

If you can't update right away, limit who holds the manage_snapshots role on your domains, and who has the es:ESHttpPut and iam:PassRole permissions for those domains. For guidance, see:

### Turnitin discloses cyberattack affecting customer-facing systems
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-24T18:59:52Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: Turnitin disclosed via a statement from its CEO, Chris Caren, that it recently detected unauthorized activity involving certain customer-facing systems. [...]
- Technical details: Turnitin disclosed via a statement from its CEO, Chris Caren, that it recently detected unauthorized activity involving certain customer-facing systems.

Educational technology company Turnitin disclosed that it detected unauthorized activity involving certain customer-facing systems and deployed its incident response plan.

In a public statement, CEO Chris Caren says that the incident is being investigated with help from external cybersecurity specialists, and law enforcement and cyber regulators have been notified.

Turnitin is a major education technology firm with a global presence, best known for its AI-generated writing detection, digital grading, academic research integrity checks, and examination proctoring.

According to its website, its software is used in over 16,000 institutions across 185 countries and serves 71 million students.

“We are writing to let you know that we recently identified unauthorized activity involving certain customer-facing Turnitin systems,” reads the letter from the CEO on the platform's website.

“Upon identifying the matter, we implemented our incident response plan, initiated an investigation with external cybersecurity specialists, and notified law enforcement and cyber regulators.”

Turnitin says its systems are fully operational and safe to use, noting that the affected systems do not collect government identification credentials or institutional financial information.

The company said its investigation into the incident remains ongoing and has not disclosed any details about the nature and scope of the unauthorized activity.

If you have any information regarding this incident or other undisclosed attacks, you can contact us confidentially via Signal at 646-961-3731 or at tips@bleepingcomputer.com.

Turnitin continues to monitor its systems for further unauthorized activity, indicating that it is still early to guarantee that the threat has been eliminated completely.

The company has not disclosed whether any user data was accessed or stolen, when the incident occurred, and how many individuals might have been impacted.

Regarding Turnitin service availability, the platform’s status page shows no incidents for November 2026, and the uptime history logs no disruptions. However, it's unclear whether it is safe to continue using the service and uploading sensitive data.

As of writing, no ransomware groups have publicly taken responsibility for the attack at Turnitin.

BleepingComputer has contacted the firm with questions about the incident, but a company representative said they have no additional details to share at this time.

See what's getting through with a 14-day read-only scan of Microsoft 365 or Google Workspace, with no MX changes.

### CVE-2026-134066 - Query injection via schema-derived identifiers in Graph Explorer
- Source: AWS Security Bulletins | Published: 2026-11-24T18:44:47Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-134066 | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: Bulletin ID: 2026-199-AWS Publication Date: 11/24/2026 10:30 AM PST Description: Graph Explorer is an open-source tool for browsing and searching graph data, which customers self-host against Amazon Neptune or other graph databases. We identified CVE-2026-134066, an issue in the query construction of Graph Explorer before version 3.2.1. Graph Explorer inserted property keys and labels read from the connected graph database into Gremlin, openCypher and SPARQL query text without escaping. A user with write access to the connected graph database could create a crafted property key or label that, when another user performs a search, causes Graph Explorer to run unintended query logic that accesses and modifies data in that database with Graph Explorer's privileges. On Amazon Neptune, the SPARQL path is not affected. Impacted versions: < 3.2.1 Refer to the article for the most up-to-date and 
- Technical details: Bulletin ID: 2026-199-AWS Scope: AWS Content Type: Important (requires attention) Publication Date: 11/24/2026 10:30 AM PST

Graph Explorer is an open-source tool for browsing and searching graph data, which customers self-host against Amazon Neptune or other graph databases.

We identified CVE-2026-134066 , an issue in the query construction of Graph Explorer before version 3.2.1. Graph Explorer inserted property keys and labels read from the connected graph database into Gremlin, openCypher and SPARQL query text without escaping. A user with write access to the connected graph database could create a crafted property key or label that, when another user performs a search, causes Graph Explorer to run unintended query logic that accesses and modifies data in that database with Graph Explorer's privileges. On Amazon Neptune, the SPARQL path is not affected.

This issue has been addressed in Graph Explorer version 3.2.1 . We recommend upgrading to the latest version and ensuring any forked or derivative code is patched to incorporate the new fixes.

If you cannot upgrade immediately, restrict write access to the graph database that Graph Explorer connects to so that only trusted principals can create or modify property keys and labels.

We would like to thank George Chen for collaborating on this issue through the coordinated vulnerability disclosure process.

Please email aws-security@amazon.com with any s ecurity questions or concerns.

### CVE-2026-134068 - Stack-based buffer overflow in replication origin name parsing in AWS pgactive
- Source: AWS Security Bulletins | Published: 2026-11-24T18:09:02Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-134068 | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: Bulletin ID: 2026-198-AWS Publication Date: 11/24/2026 10:00 AM PST Description: pgactive is an open source PostgreSQL extension from AWS that provides active-active replication between PostgreSQL databases. We identified CVE-2026-134068, an issue in the replication origin name parsing component of pgactive. Under certain circumstances, an authenticated database user with specific privileges might be able to execute arbitrary operating system commands as the database operating system user. Impacted versions: >= 2.1.0 AND < 2.1.10 Refer to the article for the most up-to-date and complete information.
- Technical details: Bulletin ID: 2026-198-AWS Scope: AWS Content Type: Important (requires attention) Publication Date: 11/24/2026 10:00 AM PST

pgactive is an open source PostgreSQL extension from AWS that provides active-active replication between PostgreSQL databases.

We identified CVE-2026-134068 , an issue in the replication origin name parsing component of pgactive. Under certain circumstances, an authenticated database user with specific privileges might be able to execute arbitrary operating system commands as the database operating system user.

This issue has been addressed in pgactive version 2.1.10 . We recommend upgrading to the latest version and ensuring any forked or derivative code is patched to incorporate the new fixes.

Until upgrading, ensure pgactive.pgactive_conflict_handlers is empty on every node and prevent new inserts. Also use only pgactive generated replication slot/origin names and never malformed, manually renamed, or overlong names. On pgactive 2.1.0, also revoke EXECUTE on pgactive.pgactive_parse_replident_name from PUBLIC.

Please email aws-security@amazon.com with any s ecurity questions or concerns.

### Radware discloses breach exposing customer cloud access keys
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-24T17:49:05Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: cloud, edge_vpn
- Summary: Cybersecurity company Radware says attackers gained access to some of its customers' account details and cloud access keys through an unsecured internal Cloud WAF (Web Application Firewall) management API that was left exposed online. [...]
- Technical details: Cybersecurity company Radware says attackers gained access to some of its customers' account details and cloud access keys through an unsecured internal Cloud WAF (Web Application Firewall) management API that was left exposed online.

Founded in 1996 and headquartered in Tel Aviv, Radware now provides application delivery and DDoS protection services to more than 12,500 enterprise and telecom carrier customers worldwide.

In a root cause analysis report shared by an anonymous source with BleepingComputer, Radware says it blocked the originating IP address and access to the internal management API. The company also revoked and replaced all internal account credentials after detecting the breach on Thursday, ten hours after the initial unauthorized API access.

"On 19 November 2026, an unauthorized party accessed an internal-only Cloud WAF management API that was reachable from the Public Internet without requiring authentication," Radware said.

"The confirmed information potentially accessed includes customer contact details, and for a subset of customers, the credentials configured for log export into their self-managed storage (AWS S3, Azure Blob Storage, or SFTP)."

The company added that the breach did not expose WAF traffic inspection, certificates, end-users’ data, customer application traffic, or cloud DDoS services and data.

It also "strongly" advised affected AppSec (WAF, BOT) customers to rotate Cloud WAF log export credentials (including Azure Blob Storage keys or SAS tokens, AWS S3 access keys, or SFTP credentials) as soon as possible and review access logs on their cloud storage accounts for unexpected access since at least November 19.

The company is still investigating the breach and whether any API keys or other customer information exposed in the incident were used without authorization, and said it has already received reports from customers about AWS alerts for an unfamiliar IP address attempting to access their log-export buckets.

"WAF traffic inspection and protection have continued without interruption, and we have not identified an impact on the availability or protection of customer applications and services," Radware added. "We have found no evidence to date of changes to customer security policies or of access to customer application traffic."

Radware has yet to issue a public statement regarding the incident or file a breach report with the US Securities and Exchange Commission.

BleepingComputer also reached out to a Radware spokesperson with questions about the incident, but a response was not immediately available.

### Iran-Aligned Volant Manticore Uses IPFS-Based KuboShell Backdoor in Espionage Campaigns
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-24T16:54:07Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: apt_intrusion, malware_botnet
- Summary: A previously undocumented Iran-aligned cyber espionage group called Volant Manticore has targeted telecommunications companies, airlines, and critical infrastructure in Cyprus. Central to the group's operations is a backdoor called KuboShell that uses the InterPlanetary File System (IPFS) for command-and-control (C2) and deploys Homa, a post-compromise framework that's a reimplementation of
- Technical details: Iran-aligned Volant Manticore targets Cyprus and Saudi Arabia using the IPFS-based KuboShell backdoor and a reworked MuddyWater framework.

A previously undocumented Iran-aligned cyber espionage group called Volant Manticore has targeted telecommunications companies, airlines, and critical infrastructure in Cyprus.

Central to the group's operations is a backdoor called KuboShell that uses the InterPlanetary File System ( IPFS ) for command-and-control (C2) and deploys Homa, a post-compromise framework that's a reimplementation of MuddyWater's MuddyC2Go . Evidence indicates that the threat actor has been operational since late August 2025.

Also delivered alongside these tools are Remote Monitoring and Management (RMM) programs like MeshCentral and NetLock RMM. In tandem, the group has been observed orchestrating credential harvesting campaigns to harvest email and corporate credentials of some of the impersonated organizations.

The use of legitimate RMM tools for persistent access has been a hallmark of the MuddyWater group , although the adversary has since also increasingly deployed its own malware in attacks. Both MuddyWater and Volant Manticore are assessed to be affiliated with Iran's Ministry of Intelligence and Security (MOIS).

"While we identified multiple links to MuddyWater, we assess that Volant Manticore likely consists of former MuddyWater operators who branched off from the group and currently operate as a separate threat cluster," Check Point said in an analysis published today. "Volant Manticore's targeting priorities appear responsive to regional geopolitical developments."

This assessment is based on the use of an older MuddyWater framework and its playbook, while diverging from established modus operandi over time to incorporate its own attack methods like credential-harvesting campaigns. The targeting of Cyprus, a country that does not align with MuddyWater's targeting patterns, and the use of separate infrastructure points to a group that pursues its own strategic objectives.

Attack chains have been found to leverage artificial intelligence (AI)-generated personas in bogus CVs to trick targets into clicking on a link to download the complete resume, resulting in the deployment of KuboShell on the victim's system. The activity is said to have been aimed at a Cyprus-based airline that operates flights between Cyprus and Israel.

This includes a shift toward Saudi Arabian healthcare entities, coinciding with escalating hostilities between Saudi Arabia and the Iranian-backed Houthis.

"This fits the MOIS's track record of targeting hospitals and healthcare providers through cyber operations during military conflicts, in some cases attempting to disrupt their operations," the cybersecurity company said.

As recently as September 2026, the group has been found to launch a phishing campaign against Saudi targets, distributing the backdoor and N-able RMM through two different healthcare-themed ZIP archives named "AI_Healthcare_Seminar.zip" and "Ai_Healthcare_Seminar_Online.zip," respectively. Some of the emails have been sent from a compromised account belonging to a private hospital in Saudi Arabia.

A Windows backdoor, KuboShell, uses IPFS for C2 and relies on Pinata to facilitate file transfer over the peer-to-peer (P2P) file-sharing network.

"On startup, KuboShell checks whether it has administrator privileges. If not, it tries to relaunch itself with higher privileges, which triggers a User Account Control (UAC) prompt," Check Point explained.

"KuboShell retrieves the MAC address of the machine's first network adapter and uses it as a unique identifier for each victim. It then registers the host by uploading a file containing hi, saved to C:\Users\Public\<MAC>.log, using a hard-coded Pinata access token."

Once registered, KuboShell checks Pinata for a command file named <MAC>.txt, and if the file exists, looks up the file’s IPFS identifier and downloads its contents through Pinata's public gateway. The downloaded content is executed via PowerShell in a hidden window. The results of the execution are saved in a file named "C:\Users\Public\<MAC>re.txt" and uploaded back to the server.

It's worth noting that the backdoor is not responsible for capturing the output. Rather, the results are returned only if the received command writes them to the text file. KuboShell then checks for a new command every 60 seconds.

Analysis indicates that KuboShell is primarily used to run basic reconnaissance commands and assess the compromised environment before downloading and executing a more capable backdoor, which includes the modular Homa Framework. This involves a three-stage process -

PowerShell stager, which contacts a hard-coded IP address ("185.205.187[.]109:8000") to fetch a PowerShell loader and execute it in memory

PowerShell loader, which decodes and launches a JavaScript backdoor
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text

### ShinyHunters claims to return after FBI arrests, uses old PGP keys as proof
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-24T16:30:19Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: ransomware
- Summary: The ShinyHunters extortion gang claims to have returned in posts on a new data leak site after a series of arrests over the past two months targeting alleged members. [...]
- Technical details: The ShinyHunters extortion gang claims to have returned in posts on a new data leak site after a series of arrests over the past two months targeting alleged members.

The new Tor site, launched on November 23, contains messages in both Russian and English announcing the group's return, distancing itself from alleged impostors, and denouncing a former member who was reportedly arrested and is cooperating with law enforcement.

The site also includes two verification messages signed using historical ShinyHunters PGP keys dating back to 2020, including one previously used to authenticate communications with BleepingComputer in August 2025.

BleepingComputer confirmed that both messages were signed using known PGP keys associated with the ShinyHunters extortion gang, confirming that the new operators have access to private signing keys historically used by the group.

However, these keys do not confirm whether the individuals behind the new operation are the same people who previously controlled the group, as the keys have circulated among numerous people.

The announcement follows a series of law enforcement operations targeting alleged ShinyHunters members after the group claimed to have breached FBI systems in September using an Oracle PeopleSoft vulnerability.

Later that month, Dutch police confirmed the arrest of a 24-year-old Amsterdam man suspected of involvement with ShinyHunters, who was identified in media reports as Pepijn van der Stap, previously known online as Umbreon.

In early October, another alleged ShinyHunters member known as Rey or Saif was reportedly detained in Jordan and is cooperating with law enforcement. Around the same time, Canadian cybersecurity executive Edward Dubrovsky was arrested in Pennsylvania in an extortion investigation reportedly linked to ShinyHunters.

After the arrests, ShinyHunters' main representative, who had regularly communicated with BleepingComputer and other media outlets, abruptly stopped responding, and their Telegram account was deleted. Soon after, the group's data leak site went offline.

In the new statement, the purported ShinyHunters operators say they are returning after months of rumors and misinformation circulated about the group.

"We’re finally back. A lot has happened recently, and there’s still plenty going on right now," reads the English announcement.

"To be honest, there’s just too much nonsense going on. Everyone’s telling different stories, but no one is really explaining what’s been happening over the last few months."

The operators of the new data leak site say they will communicate exclusively through the Exploit hacking forum account, which they claim to have controlled since 2020, and through the new Tor site.

The statement also addresses the reported arrest of a hacker and ShinyHunters member known as Rey, Dedale, or Saif, who was reportedly detained in Jordan in late September.

According to the announcement, Rey previously collaborated with ShinyHunters but began operating independently, creating Telegram channels without the group's approval.

"As far as we know, he has already been arrested and is now "actively cooperating" with the authorities," reads the English message.

"We already rotated the PGP key that Rey used for DLS, since it has most likely been compromised."

### European Commission confirms data theft in EU Academy breach
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-24T16:22:44Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: The European Commission, the European Union's main executive body, has confirmed that attackers breached its EU Academy online learning hub and stole the personal data of an undisclosed number of users. [...]
- Technical details: The European Commission, the European Union's main executive body, has confirmed that attackers breached its EU Academy online learning hub and stole the personal data of an undisclosed number of users.

This follows the Commission's Thursday disclosure that hackers breached its systems on October 22 using the ARTEX AI-powered penetration testing framework , causing service disruptions that also affected its internal Sysper HR platform.

"As a protective and preventive measure, we temporarily took down several ​applications and most of them have since been restored. The investigation is still ongoing," spokesperson Thomas Regnier told reporters at the time. "We are taking any necessary steps based on the findings as they come. We have not detected any evidence of a data breach affecting Commission staff as a whole."

While the Commission said it hasn't received a ransom demand and has yet to attribute the attack, Regnier confirmed in a Monday press briefing that the attackers also breached the EU Academy platform, used to learn more about EU policies and follow EU online courses.

Regnier also confirmed a data leak following the EU Academy incident, which is still under investigation and has affected users' personal information, including their names and email addresses.

"And as a precautionary measure, given that the fact that there is a data leak that is confirmed including names and email addresses,today there was a message to all users on the EU academy website," he added. "This is definitely a cyber attack, a cyber campaign that was targeted against the Commission via an AI assisted tool. When it comes to the attribution, we would need the investigation to be concluded to be able to find out who is behind this."

The Commission has also published a notice on the EU Academy website about this cybersecurity incident, informing users that public access to the platform has been disabled and warning them to be suspicious of unsolicited emails or messages that may be part of potential phishing attacks.

The European Commission disclosed another data breach in February after discovering on January 30 that a mobile device management platform used to manage staff devices had been compromised.

More recently, it disclosed an additional incident on March 27 after BleepingComputer reached out for confirmation that the Commission's Amazon cloud environment had been breached. The EU's Cybersecurity Service (CERT-EU) later attributed the March cloud hack to the TeamPCP threat group and said the resulting breach exposed the data of at least 29 other Union entities.

These recent security breaches follow the Commission's January 20 proposal for new cybersecurity legislation to strengthen defenses against cybercrime groups and state-backed actors targeting Europe's critical infrastructure.

### Hackers compromise Check Point Servers in 22 organizations
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-24T16:15:23Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2020-1472, CVE-2021-21972, CVE-2021-26855, CVE-2023-34048, CVE-2023-4966, CVE-2024-21893, CVE-2025-5777, CVE-2026-16232, CVE-2026-41089, CVE-2026-61500, CVE-2026-98432 | Topic signals: apt_intrusion
- Summary: Threat actors have exploited multiple vulnerabilities to compromise two dozen Check Point Security management servers in an ongoing campaign targeting 22 organizations. [...]
- Technical details: Threat actors have exploited multiple vulnerabilities to compromise two dozen Check Point Security management servers in an ongoing campaign targeting 22 organizations.

Threat intelligence firm GreyNoise detected the activity, noting that it resembled cyberespionage operations, although the attacker's motivation could not be conclusively determined.

The researchers track the activity as GT13 and confirmed it was ongoing between October 17 and November 2, when their visibility ended. However, given the threat actor's persistent access to Check Point devices, they believe the campaign remains active.

GreyNoise says the 22 victim organizations are spread across 17 countries, and show no clear evidence of specific targeting. They include government entities, financial services providers, and companies in the energy, transportation, mining, and manufacturing sectors.

The attacks were launched from 99 IP addresses that appear to come from compromised Check Point devices and ExpressVPN exit nodes.

A Rejetto HTTP File Server, compromised by exploiting CVE-2026-61500, was used as an operational relay for managing intrusions and collecting stolen data from the compromised environments.

While GreyNoise could not determine when or how the Check Point servers were breached, it observed the exploitation of CVE-2026-16232 during the post-compromise stages.

The vulnerability is an authentication bypass in Check Point SmartConsole that allows attackers to obtain an application login token and authenticate with administrator privileges.

Check Point warned about the flaw in July, marking it as an actively exploited zero-day .

The researchers also observed attempts to exploit CVE-2025-5777 (Citrix NetScaler), CVE-2024-21893 (Ivanti), CVE-2026-41089 (Windows NetLogon), CVE-2026-16232 (Check Point SmartConsole auth bypass to Run Script), and CVE-2026-98432 (Linux IPv4 UAF).

GreyNoise recorded over 1,400 network sessions, 800 command-line artifacts, and 1,200 file-related activities, such as:

Stealing firewall databases, credentials, configuration data, and internal certificate authority files.

Generating a new certificate in one victim's environment.

Unloading security policies, effectively disabling firewall protections.

Conducting internal network reconnaissance and attempting lateral movement.

Targeting additional enterprise systems, including Citrix NetScaler (CVE-2023-4966), Ivanti Connect Secure (CVE-2024-21893), VMware vCenter (CVE-2021-21972 and CVE-2023-34048), Microsoft Exchange (CVE-2021-26855/34473), and Microsoft Netlogon (CVE-2020-1472).

On October 26, the attacker performed a bulk certificate management operation across all 24 compromised Check Point servers, which failed, but still helped GreyNoise identify victims.

### How to compare MDR providers when every proposal looks promising
- Source: Rapid7 Research | Published: 2026-11-24T15:51:25Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: Choosing a managed detection and response (MDR) provider means committing budget, connecting systems, and trusting another team to act when your organization is under pressure. Yet a proposal promising 24x7 coverage, expert analysts, and fast response can leave important questions unanswered. Which systems will actually be monitored? What will analysts investigate before contacting you? What happens when an incident needs more work? Before choosing a provider or renewing your current agreement, ask for evidence of how the service operates. Rapid7’s MDR Evaluation Scorecard gives you 18 checks to structure that conversation and record what still needs proving. Start with one investigation Ask a provider to walk you through a completed investigation, using an anonymized example where necessary. Follow the initial signal through the evidence considered, the analyst’s decision, and any respo
- Technical details: Choosing a managed detection and response (MDR) provider means committing budget, connecting systems, and trusting another team to act when your organization is under pressure. Before choosing a provider or renewing your current agreement, consult Rapid7’s MDR Evaluation Scorecard: 18 checks to structure your conversation and record what still needs proving.

Choosing a managed detection and response (MDR) provider means committing budget, connecting systems, and trusting another team to act when your organization is under pressure. Yet a proposal promising 24x7 coverage, expert analysts, and fast response can leave important questions unanswered. Which systems will actually be monitored? What will analysts investigate before contacting you? What happens when an incident needs more work?

Before choosing a provider or renewing your current agreement, ask for evidence of how the service operates. Rapid7’s MDR Evaluation Scorecard gives you 18 checks to structure that conversation and record what still needs proving.

Ask a provider to walk you through a completed investigation, using an anonymized example where necessary. Follow the initial signal through the evidence considered, the analyst’s decision, and any response actions.

Suppose suspicious activity involves an account with access to a critical business system. Can the provider show how the account’s permissions, the systems within reach, and known weaknesses influenced its investigation? Can it explain what it ruled out and why it escalated or contained the activity?

This exercise helps you assess whether exposure context, intelligence, and customer telemetry work together. It also makes the division of work visible: what the provider investigated, what it decided, and what your team had to do next.

Use the same requirements for every provider, including your incumbent. Confirm the proposed service tier, supported integrations, response responsibilities, and total costs before comparing scores.

Be specific about incident response. Establish who handles containment, forensics, eradication, and mitigation, which actions require your approval, and whether additional work changes the bill. A demonstration should be supported by written commitments about the service you would receive.

Rapid7 MDR includes unlimited incident response, giving customers direct access to senior responders for digital forensics, eradication, and mitigation. When evaluating that commitment, ask us to explain the activities covered, how containment and remediation are coordinated, and which decisions remain with your team.

Apply the same scrutiny to intelligence. Ask for an example showing how an emerging threat led to detection updates, hunting activity, or customer guidance. Rapid7’s emerging-threat response process connects research, detection engineering, product, and communications. The useful evaluation question is how that process changes the protection and advice customers receive.

A detailed investigation record helps you understand what happened. Your next question should be what changed afterward.

Look for recommendations that led to stronger controls, better detections, or closed coverage gaps. Ask how the provider reviews completed actions and recurring problems with your team. When comparing reporting, check whether you can trace those improvements alongside investigation volumes and response measures.

The scorecard covers six areas: exposure-aware detection, intelligence, response ownership and cost, transparency, team expertise, and integration. Rate each capability against evidence, then record limitations, outstanding questions, and follow-up owners.

The total helps organize your comparison, but essential requirements deserve individual attention. A strong overall score cannot compensate for a response capability your organization needs and the proposed service excludes.

Rapid7s curated database of vulnerabilities, featuring exploit modules and check methods integrated into the Metasploit Framework.

The threat research behind the alerts: adversary tracking, curated intelligence, and flagship threat reports.

Gain 24x7 XDR monitoring, remediation, and DFIR from experts that extend your team to help secure your extended ecosystem.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text

The system must be rebooted for this update to take effect.

Red Hat recommends treating all kernel errata as security-relevant. Given the kernel's fundamental role, any bug has a higher chance of impacting system security, even if that impact only becomes clear after a fix is published. Therefore, Red Hat prioritizes delivering fixes that improve our customers' overall security posture.

Because of this proactive approach, a patch may be associated with a CVE assignment at a future date. Retroactive CVE assignments are always documented in the corresponding errata and on Red Hat's CVE pages. We strongly advise against delaying updates, as doing so may leave your system exposed when protections are already available.

### Police disrupt 57 illegal streaming services with 7.3 million visits
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-24T15:10:10Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: Law enforcement authorities from 11 countries have disrupted 57 illegal streaming services that operated hundreds of websites and drew over 7.3 million visits each year. [...]
- Technical details: Law enforcement authorities from 11 countries have disrupted 57 illegal streaming services that operated hundreds of websites and drew over 7.3 million visits each year.

Such illegal IPTV services offer access to television channels, live sports, and movies without permission from the copyright owners for a small monthly fee.

According to the European Audiovisual Anti-Piracy Alliance (AAPA) trade association, legitimate service providers lose around €4.83 billion annually as roughly 60 million people in Europe use illegal platforms for streaming content.

"The operation resulted in the disruption of 57 illegal IPTV services and the identification of 321 websites illegally offering copyrighted content, with a combined annual traffic of more than 7.3 million visits. Investigators have so far traced over EUR 26 million in cryptocurrency transactions, with further analysis ongoing," Europol said in a Tuesday press release.

"The operation targeted a criminal market worth billions of euros annually, in which illegal streaming providers sell unauthorised access to premium television channels, live sports and films, often collecting subscription payments through cryptocurrency."

As part of this joint action, which also involved Europol, the European Union Intellectual Property Office (EUIPO), INTERPOL, and AAPA, the investigators also partnered with crypto industry experts from Binance, Coinbase, and Chainalysis to trace cryptocurrency payments and identify the financial networks powering these illegal IPTV services.

They did this by looking for links between the illegal IPTV services, crypto wallets, financial transactions, and the individuals who control these services, who used the wallets to receive and transfer payments, making it easier to move money between countries and hide the origin of their profits.

Europol and the European Union Intellectual Property Office (EUIPO) joined forces in 2016 to create the Intellectual Property Crime Coordinated Coalition (IPC3) , which operates within Europol and targets counterfeiting and online and offline piracy.

In June, another major international operation coordinated by Bulgaria with Europol's support, dubbed "Operation KRATOS 2," resulted in the removal of more than 27,000 illegal streaming URLs , the shutdown of nine organized crime groups, and 29 arrests.

That followed Operation KRATOS , another international anti-piracy operation carried out in summer 2024, which shut down a massive illegal streaming network with over 22 million users worldwide.

Earlier this year, in January, Europol, Eurojust, and Interpol also coordinated Operation Switch Off , a global law enforcement action that led to the seizure of three industrial-scale illegal IPTV services.

More recently, in May, Italian authorities dismantled the CINEMAGOAL piracy platform , which provided illegal access to many online streaming platforms, including Netflix, Spotify, and Disney+.

Adaptive Security's AI agents analyze email intent, context, and behavior to uncover phishing, business email compromise, and invoice fraud.

See what's getting through with a 14-day read-only scan of Microsoft 365 or Google Workspace, with no MX changes.

Police dismantles 9 crime groups in illegal streaming crackdown

Dizigom illegal streaming service with 21M monthly visits shuts down

Police arrests 270 dark web vendors, buyers in global crackdown

### From Vulnerability Noise to Resilience: Rethinking Security in the AI Era
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-24T15:01:11Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: cloud, ai_threat, supply_chain
- Summary: AI-driven development is adding complexity to software supply chains, leaving security teams overwhelmed by vulnerabilities that may pose little actual risk. RapidFort explains how runtime visibility, container hardening, and automated remediation can reduce vulnerability noise and improve software resilience. [...]
- Technical details: AI-driven development is adding complexity to software supply chains, leaving security teams overwhelmed by vulnerabilities that may pose little actual risk. RapidFort explains how runtime visibility, container hardening, and automated remediation can reduce vulnerability noise and improve software resilience.

Written by: George Manuelian, Chief Strategy Officer, RapidFort

The modern software stack has become an iceberg. The application code customers and security teams see at the surface is only a small portion of what is being shipped. Beneath it sit operating systems, frameworks, libraries, packages, and transitive dependencies, with much of it drawn from open source or third parties.

As AI accelerates software development, that hidden complexity has become an even more critical security challenge.

For business and technology leaders, the question is no longer simply how many vulnerabilities exist. It is where does meaningful risk live, which vulnerabilities can be exploited in our environment, and how we can reduce that risk without turning developers into full-time vulnerability managers.

The answer requires a fundamental shift from vulnerability accumulation to continuous software resilience.

The typical containerized stack is layers of base operating system software, packages, libraries, language frameworks and dependencies, and the application itself. The application a company writes may represent only a small portion of what it ultimately ships.

That is important because vulnerabilities multiply. A vulnerable package in a base image is not necessarily one vulnerability in practice; it can be replicated across every application and container that uses that image.

In light of this, executives should ask a different question of their security programs: How much of the reported risk is relevant to the way our software operates? Traditional scanning frequently identifies the presence of a vulnerable component without establishing whether that component is loaded, executing, reachable or exposed.

A software bill of materials (SBOM) identifies what exists; a runtime bill of materials (RBOM) can pinpoint what is actually being used. That distinction can dramatically reduce noise.

For example, in the real world from a customer, an NGINX image containing 152 packages and 48 CVEs had only 47 packages actually running in the application. More than 80% of the software was not being exercised. The lesson is not that every unused package is harmless, but that security teams need operational context before assigning remediation urgency.

This is also why severity alone has become an inadequate basis for prioritization. A high or critical Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) score can command attention, but the business question is whether the vulnerable code is relevant and exploitable in the environment.

There are four contextual factors increasingly shaping remediation decisions: Whether a vulnerability is exposed, whether it is a known exploited vulnerability, whether exploitation is automatable, and whether exploitation would have meaningful technical impact.

The shift from severity to exploitability and reachability is important because it changes the operating model. Instead of asking engineering teams to chase the largest number on a scanner dashboard, leaders should ask whether the organization has enough visibility to determine what is running, what is reachable, and what is exposed.

As a result, security becomes a business-risk exercise rather than a race to close tickets.

RapidFort Curated Images give your team 35,000+ near-zero CVE container images, rebuilt and hardened every 24 hours.

Swap in a hardened base image and remove exploitable vulnerabilities at the source, with no code, OS or pipeline changes.

Hardening should not mean merely passing a configuration benchmark. It should also mean removing software and functionality that the application does not need

### Recent Trends and Tactics of Agentic AI Attacks
- Source: Palo Alto Unit 42 | Published: 2026-11-24T15:00:19Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: apt_intrusion, ai_threat
- Summary: We tracked campaigns where attackers deployed autonomous AI agents to automate multi-platform intrusions, using standard tools while leaving distinct operational traces. The post Recent Trends and Tactics of Agentic AI Attacks appeared first on Unit 42 .
- Technical details: We tracked campaigns where attackers deployed autonomous AI agents to automate multi-platform intrusions, using standard tools while leaving distinct operational traces.

Attackers are weaponizing autonomous AI agents in real-world campaigns, creating a risk that until recently has only been theoretical. Between July and September 2026, we identified and tracked more than 10 global campaigns in which different attackers deployed autonomous AI agents to enhance their attacks. These agentic AI attackers operated at machine speed, compressing the attack lifecycle from weeks or days into mere hours.

These campaigns targeted diverse sectors, including government, logistics, hospitality, manufacturing, agriculture and education. In every observed campaign the attackers gained their initial foothold through web shells that were deployed via vulnerable web applications.

The novelty of the observed agentic campaigns was not in their techniques, but in their speed and efficiency. Following the path of least resistance with familiar open-source tools and lightweight custom scripts, the AI-driven operators matched the tradecraft of proficient human attackers while operating at machine scale, at times executing hundreds of unique commands in just minutes.

However, using AI agents came at the expense of operational stealth. The agents left unmistakable behavioral fingerprints that revealed the involvement of non-human operators, including:

Chain-of-thought reasoning comments that appeared in shell commands

This article details the real-world scenarios that we observed, organized by the tactical patterns that define how agentic AI is being weaponized today.

Palo Alto Networks customers are better protected from the threats discussed above through the following products and services:

Advanced URL Filtering , Advanced DNS Security and Advanced IP Defense

Next-Generation Firewall with the Advanced Threat Prevention

The Unit 42 AI Security Assessment service can help identify and mitigate complex AI-enabled risks.

Unit 42 Continuous Frontier AI Defense is an offensive security service designed to assist organizations with continuously discovering hidden and unknown exposures in dynamic environments, validate real-world exploitability across attack paths through agentic testing and expert analysis, and provide prioritized remediation and virtual patching guidance to accelerate risk mitigation.

Organizations can access tailored adversary intelligence, direct analyst consultations, and specialized exposure tracking through Unit 42 Threat Intel Services and its Deep and Dark Web Service .

If you think you might have been compromised or have an urgent matter, contact the Unit 42 Incident Response team .

An agentic AI attacker is an autonomous AI system — usually a large language model (LLM) with tool-calling capabilities — that independently plans, executes and adapts attack sequences without continuous human guidance.remote

Unlike chatbots that respond to individual prompts, agentic AI systems operate in a loop : they observe their environment, reason about what to do next, take action and then observe the results to inform their next step.

The weaponization of agentic AI represents an increased threat to security for several reasons:

Speed and scale: An agentic AI attacker operates at machine speed. In the campaigns we observed, AI agents executed hundreds of unique commands within minutes.

Lowered barrier to entry: Attackers can simply provide high-level guidance while the AI agent automates intricate technical phases. This includes complex actions such as compiling kernel exploits, navigating cross-platform lateral movement and systematically compromising cloud infrastructure.

### McDonald's Japan data breach exposes customer location information
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-24T14:09:37Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: McDonald's Japan, the fast-food giant's Japanese subsidiary, has disclosed a data breach after attackers accessed a database containing sensitive information for users of the company's official smartphone app. [...]
- Technical details: McDonald's Japan, the fast-food giant's Japanese subsidiary, has disclosed a data breach after attackers accessed a database containing sensitive information for users of the company's official smartphone app.

Founded in 1940, McDonald's is now a multinational fast-food chain that operates more than 40,000 restaurants in more than 100 countries and serves tens of millions of customers daily.

In a Friday statement, the company's Japanese subsidiary confirmed that threat actors gained access to its official app's database and said the exposed information includes the users' product purchase history, IP addresses, mobile device location data, and customer management IDs (which cannot be used to identify the customers' email addresses or member IDs).

McDonald's Japan also added that the exposed data doesn't include the affected customers' names, email addresses, phone numbers, home addresses, or credit card information.

While it has yet to reveal when it detected the attack or how many individuals had their data stolen, the company said a follow-up investigation found no service disruption or misuse of official apps as a result of the incident.

"We are working to invalidate any credentials used for unauthorized access, investigate the relevant systems, and report to the Personal Information Protection Commission," it said . "In addition, we will strive to strengthen security measures and prevent recurrence."

This isn't the first data breach to affect McDonald's in recent years, with the fast food giant disclosing a breach in June 2021 after attackers breached its systems and stole information belonging to employees and customers from the US, South Korea, and Taiwan.

In 2017, McDonald's also patched a cross-site scripting (XSS) security flaw that affected its official website and exposed customers' plain-text passwords .

Last month, Burger King Japan also disclosed a major data breach after attackers stole 11.18 million customer records that were later leaked online.

The two incidents affecting the two Japanese fast food chains add to a large wave of cyberattacks impacting high-profile Japanese companies since the start of the year, including Japan Airlines , entertainment system maker Daiichi Kosho , cloud and digital infrastructure firm IDC Frontier , Japanese publishing giant Nikkei , cloud and data center service provider Sakura Internet , taxi operator Nihon Kotsu , telecommunications giant KDDI , and many others.

Macnica researcher Yutaka Sejiyama has logged 288 cybersecurity incidents affecting Japanese organizations involving personal information theft or exposure since the start of the year.

Sejiyama found that attackers are now probing websites and APIs for access-control, configuration, and authentication weaknesses, and exploiting known (n-day) vulnerabilities to find other victims.

- Technical details: Multiple vulnerabilities in ClamAV could allow a remote attacker to bypass configured file and scan time limits and cause a denial of service (DoS) condition, interrupting scanning operations. Notes: The Security Impact Rating (SIR) for these vulnerabilities is High only for Windows-based platforms because those platforms run the ClamAV scanning process in a privileged security context. The platforms that are highly impacted include Cisco Secure Endpoint Connector for Windows. The SIR for these vulnerabilities is Medium on other platforms, including Linux and Mac platforms, because those platforms run the ClamAV scanning process in a lower-privileged security context. The affected platforms include Secure Endpoint Connector for Linux and Mac. Cisco Secure Endpoint Private Cloud itself is not impacted by these vulnerabilities. Instead, the Cisco Secure Endpoint Connector software is impacted. Multiple vulnerabilities in ClamAV could allow a remote attacker to bypass configured file and scan time limits and cause a denial of service (DoS) condition, interrupting scanning operations.

For more information about these vulnerabilities, see the Details section of this advisory.

For additional information on these vulnerabilities in ClamAV, see the ClamAV blog .

Cisco has released software updates that address these vulnerabilities in affected Cisco platforms. There are no workarounds that address these vulnerabilities.

The Security Impact Rating (SIR) for these vulnerabilities is High only for Windows-based platforms because those platforms run the ClamAV scanning process in a privileged security context. The platforms that are highly impacted include Cisco Secure Endpoint Connector for Windows.

The SIR for these vulnerabilities is Medium on other platforms, including Linux and Mac platforms, because those platforms run the ClamAV scanning process in a lower-privileged security context. The affected platforms include Secure Endpoint Connector for Linux and Mac.

Cisco Secure Endpoint Private Cloud itself is not impacted by these vulnerabilities. Instead, the Cisco Secure Endpoint Connector software is impacted.

This advisory is available at the following link: https://sec.cloudapps.cisco.com/security/center/content/CiscoSecurityAdvisory/cisco-sa-clamav-multi-e93M3vdA

The following table lists Cisco products that are affected by the vulnerabilities that are described in this advisory. Customers should refer to the associated Cisco bug IDs for further details.

Cisco products may be impacted differently depending on implementation and usage of ClamAV. For information on the effects of these vulnerabilities on specific Cisco products, see the Details section of this advisory.

Only products listed in the Vulnerable Products section of this advisory are known to be affected by these vulnerabilities.

The vulnerabilities are not dependent on one another. Exploitation of one of the vulnerabilities is not required to exploit another vulnerability. In addition, a software release that is affected by one of the vulnerabilities may not be affected by the other vulnerabilities.

CVE-2026-76411: ClamAV Phishcheck Feature DoS Vulnerability

A vulnerability in the phishcheck functionality of ClamAV could allow an unauthenticated, remote attacker to cause a DoS condition on an affected device.

This vulnerability is due to improper allocation of memory when URLs are processed. An attacker could exploit this vulnerability by sending a crafted URL to be processed by the software. A successful exploit could allow the attacker to cause the ClamAV scanning process to terminate, resulting in a DoS condition on the affected software.

Cisco has released software updates that address this vulnerability. There are no workarounds that address this vulnerability.

Bug ID(s): CSCwv98582 CVE ID: CVE-2026-76411 Security Impact Rating (SIR): High CVSS Base Score: 7.5 CVSS Vector: CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H

CVE-2026-76414: ClamAV Microsoft Compiled HTML Help File Infinite Loop Vulnerability

Red Hat recommends treating all kernel errata as security-relevant. ### New Shirudo tool uses AI to shield unpatched Linux systems from exploits
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-24T13:34:23Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: active_exploitation, ai_threat
- Summary: OpenAI and Cisco launched an open-source Linux security tool named Shirudo that deploys temporary runtime protections to block vulnerability exploitation while administrators prepare and roll out patches, without restarting services. [...]
- Technical details: OpenAI and Cisco launched an open-source Linux security tool named Shirudo that deploys temporary runtime protections to block vulnerability exploitation while administrators prepare and roll out patches, without restarting services.

Shirudo leverages the eBPF (extended Berkeley Packet Filter) mechanism in Linux to monitor for suspicious activity and deploy targeted protections called "shields."

eBPF is a Linux technology that lets programs run in a controlled environment within the operating system's kernel without modifying its source code or requiring a restart.

It enables real-time monitoring of system and network activity, performance analysis, and enforcement of targeted security protections, making it an ideal operational basis for Shirudo.

Shirudo’s shields aim to block malicious behavior in a targeted way, leaving legitimate application operations undisturbed. OpenAI highlights four core capabilities for the tool:

Live protection: Blocks vulnerable code paths without restarting systems, letting operators assess shield impact before enforcing them.

AI-assisted defense: Enables agents to investigate vulnerabilities, develop shields, and validate their effectiveness, with deployment remaining under human control.

Trusted deployment: Uses cryptographic signatures, Linux's eBPF verifier, and kernel security controls to prevent unauthorized modifications.

Hardware-backed verification: Uses Trusted Platform Modules (TPMs) to provide verifiable records of shields being loaded or removed.

OpenAI and Cisco researchers tested custom-generated shields and demonstrated that they could successfully block a Java4Shell exploit attempt against a running Elasticsearch service without interrupting its operation.

Marcus Hutchins, principal threat researcher at managed detection and response company Expel, says that while there is "genuinely so much runtime exploit mitigation potential with eBPF," it may not be a good idea to let AI agents write rules that could impact the kernel.

He also noted that the tool's "capabilities are limited to Linux because Mac/Windows haven't implement the components needed to be effective."

Researchers at Isovalent, a cloud networking and security company acquired by Cisco in 2024, explain that "Shirudo is a rewrite of Tetragon," an open-source eBPF-based runtime security platform the company introduced in 2022, which also serves as the foundation for Cisco's existing Live Protect technology.

Shirudo is designed for agentic control plane, and a configurable degree of autonomy when it comes to deploying mitigations. Isovalent says the tool isn’t limited to eBPF and can also modify application or userspace runtime bytecode to extend protection where needed.

When a shield is deployed, they also provide operators with hit counts, giving them insight into attempted exploits and where defenses need strengthening.

The two companies plan to provide “ready-to-use” mitigations in the form of tested shields, along with vulnerability disclosures, until patching is possible. Shields will be distributed as cryptographically signed ‘.bee’ packages through an OCI registry.

Isovalent says that Shirudo can be activated through a command-line interface and doesn’t require a daemon to run permanently on the machine.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text
- EPSS context: EPSS unavailable / not applicable — FIRST.org; not an asset-specific risk score
