### VMware vCenter Hit by Another Critical 9.8-Rated Auth Bypass Flaw After Zero-Day Exploitation
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-24T13:28:26Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-123890, CVE-2026-123903, CVE-2026-123904, CVE-2026-123905, CVE-2026-123906, CVE-2026-123907, CVE-2026-123908 | Topic signals: active_exploitation, identity
- Summary: Broadcom has shipped patches to address 23 vulnerabilities impacting VMware ESX, vCenter, Workstation, Fusion, and Avi Load Balancer, including one critical bug that could be abused to bypass authentication. The critical vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-123903, is rated 9.8 out of 10.0 on the CVSS scoring system. It resides in the VMware Directory Service ("vmdir"). "A malicious actor with
- Technical details: Broadcom patches 23 VMware flaws, including a critical vCenter authentication bypass that could allow unauthorized access to the system.

Broadcom has shipped patches to address 23 vulnerabilities impacting VMware ESX, vCenter, Workstation, Fusion, and Avi Load Balancer, including one critical bug that could be abused to bypass authentication.

The critical vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-123903 , is rated 9.8 out of 10.0 on the CVSS scoring system. It resides in the VMware Directory Service ("vmdir").

"A malicious actor with network access to vCenter may exploit this issue to bypass authentication and gain unauthorized access to the system," Broadcom said in an advisory released today.

The company credited sglab of QiAnXin, Qi Qin (@LeoQ7_) of depthfirst, and VulTeam and XGTP from ThreatBook for independently reporting the flaw.

Also patched in vCenter are four high-severity flaws, including an arbitrary file write vulnerability while processing certain archives that could be exploited to achieve code execution on the appliance (CVE-2026-123904, CVSS score: 7.5).

The remaining three vulnerabilities relate to denial-of-service in the VMware Directory Service (CVE-2026-123905, CVE-2026-123906, and CVE-2026-123907, CVSS scores: 7.5).

The issues have been addressed in the following versions -

VMware Cloud Foundation and VMware vSphere Foundation 9.1.x.x - Fixed in 9.1.2

VMware Cloud Foundation and VMware vSphere Foundation 9.0.x.x - Fixed in 9.0.2.0300

VMware vCenter 7.0 (Contact Broadcom Support, if on extended support contract)

VMware Telco Cloud Platform - Fixed in vCenter 8.0 U3m (TCI: 3.0, TCP: 3.0, 4.x, 5.0.x, 5.1.x, 5.2)

VMware Telco Cloud Infrastructure - Fixed in vCenter 8.0 U3m (TCI: 3.0, TCP: 3.0, 4.x, 5.0.x, 5.1.x, 5.2)

The fixes arrive days after Broadcom shipped updates for CVE-2026-123890 (CVSS score: 9.8), another authentication bypass flaw impacting the VMware Directory Service that was exploited as a zero-day by a suspected China-nexus cyber espionage group called Armory Castle. VMware has noted that CVE-2026-123903 is a distinct issue and is not related to CVE-2026-123890.

While Broadcom makes no mention of any of the newly disclosed flaws as being exploited in the wild, it's essential that customers apply the updates for optimal protection.
- Fix / remediation: Also patched in vCenter are four high-severity flaws, including an arbitrary file write vulnerability while processing certain archives that could be exploited to achieve code execution on the appliance (CVE-2026-123904, CVSS score: 7.5). VMware Cloud Foundation and VMware vSphere Foundation 9.1.x.x - Fixed in 9.1.2 VMware Cloud Foundation and VMware vSphere Foundation 9.0.x.x - Fixed in 9.0.2.0300 VMware Telco Cloud Platform - Fixed in vCenter 8.0 U3m (TCI: 3.0, TCP: 3.0, 4.x, 5.0.x, 5.1.x, 5.2) VMware Telco Cloud Infrastructure - Fixed in vCenter 8.0 U3m (TCI: 3.0, TCP: 3.0, 4.x, 5.0.x, 5.1.x, 5.2) Resolution: To remediate CVE-2026-123903 apply the patches listed in the 'Fixed Version' column of the 'Response Matrix' found below. Resolution: To remediate CVE-2026-123904 apply the patches listed in the 'Fixed Version' column of the 'Response Matrix' found below.

### Okta Links ShinyHunters-Associated Vishing Group to New INCEPTION Data Leak Site
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-24T13:28:21Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: ransomware, identity, phishing
- Summary: A threat cluster previously assessed to be affiliated with the ShinyHunters extortion crew may have rebranded and continued operations under a new name called INCEPTION, according to Okta's Threat Intelligence team. The cluster, dubbed O-UNC-053, is characterized by the use of voice phishing (aka vishing) attacks to trick targets into entering credentials in phishing pages and approving
- Technical details: Okta links an active vishing cluster previously associated with ShinyHunters to the INCEPTION leak site, but its operators remain unconfirmed.

A threat cluster previously assessed to be affiliated with the ShinyHunters extortion crew may have rebranded and continued operations under a new name called INCEPTION , according to Okta's Threat Intelligence team.

The cluster, dubbed O-UNC-053 , is characterized by the use of voice phishing (aka vishing) attacks to trick targets into entering credentials in phishing pages and approving multi-factor authentication (MFA). Okta said it's been tracking the group since March 2026.

The identity and access management provider said targets of this cluster previously had stolen data published to the ShinyHunters data leak site (DLS). However, as of November 16, 2026, a new data leak site operating under the name INCEPTION has listed the data siphoned from O-UNC-053 targets.

"While this threat cluster has changed their brand affiliation, the operators remain active in targeting organizations with voice phishing attacks and account takeovers resulting in data theft and extortion," Okta researchers Moussa Diallo and Katie Nickels said in a report shared with The Hacker News.

Social engineering and credential harvesting attacks mounted by O-UNC-053 have targeted a broad range of sectors, including technology, healthcare, biotechnology, financial services, insurance, entertainment, gaming, retail, transportation, hospitality, and legal.

Similar to O-UNC-066 and UNC6671 , the newly identified cluster also relies on a phishing kit to intercept authentication flows and capture a user's credentials and MFA codes. However, the kit is not designed to bypass phishing-resistant methods like passkeys and Okta FastPass.

"While interacting with targeted users on the phone, the operators socially engineer them into sharing factors like one-time passcodes and approving push notifications," Okta said. "If successful, the operators gain access to SaaS applications and exfiltrate sensitive data, which is often used to extort targets under the threat of releasing it on a data leak site."

Domains created by the group are registered through NiceNIC, Spaceship, Tucows, and NameSilo, with the domain names using the following patterns -

The use of "legal" themes in these attacks is said to have started in October 2026, and is often registered on or within a day of a campaign starting. Prior campaign waves have employed help desk-, support-, and claims-related themes for the registered domains.

These findings also dovetail with an alert from Palo Alto Networks Unit 42, which has warned of widespread vishing activity using legal-themed domains that either begin with "legal-" or end with "-legal" using the ".com" top-level domain.

More than 70 such domains have been observed since October 2026, it said, adding each domain is specifically created for an intended target operating in different industries, like cryptocurrency, financial services, healthcare, high technology, and hospitality, among others.

"Registered in rapid evening batches and shielded behind Cloudflare, these web-only domains deploy a filtered kit that delivers tailored content to intended victims while routing visitors it is not configured to attack to the targeted organization's legitimate Okta or NetIQ sign-in flow," ReversingLabs researcher Robert Simmons said .

Some of the confirmed domains linked to the activity are listed below -

The crux of the campaign is to tailor the landing page to the specific identity provider of the target, while filtering unwanted visitors and forwarding them to the organization's authentication infrastructure, such as Okta.

"Throughout our tracking of O-UNC-053 between March and September 2026, we observed the names of targeted entities appear on the ShinyHunters DLS between 1 and 28 days after the phishing infrastructure was registered," Okta noted.

However, it's unclear at this stage if the published data on the INCEPTION site is authentic and whether they are running the site themselves or are collaborating with another extortion gang. The exact nature of the relationship between ShinyHunters and O-UNC-053 is also unknown.

It's worth noting that the ShinyHunters data leak site went offline in late September 2026 following the arrest of three alleged members of the group, including a Canadian co-conspirator responsible for the breach of the FBIjobs.gov portal.

There is no evidence of data from O-UNC-053 targets being published on any data leak site from early September 2026 through October and early November 2026, until listings for multiple targets appeared on the INCEPTION site last week.

### Rapid7 Analysis: VMware vCenter SRP Authentication Bypass to Root (CVE-2026-123890)
- Source: Rapid7 Research | Published: 2026-11-24T13:01:25Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-123890 | Topic signals: identity, cloud
- Summary: Overview An unauthenticated attacker can retrieve the session key for an in-progress administrator login to VMware vCenter's directory service. The attacker then uses that key to complete the login without knowing the account's password. The vulnerable interface is exposed through DCE/RPC on TCP port 2012. The issue, tracked as CVE-2026-123890 , combines a Secure Remote Password (SRP) session-key getter that is callable before authentication with an RPC context lookup that does not enforce ownership. The context handles use predictable UUIDv1 values. By creating two reference contexts around a pending login, an attacker can narrow the hidden login handle to a short timestamp window and ask the server for its key. We reproduced the bypass against vCenter Server Appliance 8.0.2.00400, build 23929136 , running Photon OS 4.0, and leveraged the resulting administrator connection through to ro
- Technical details: An unauthenticated attacker can retrieve the session key for an in-progress administrator login to VMware vCenter's directory service. The attacker then uses that key to complete the login without knowing the account's password. The vulnerable interface is exposed through DCE/RPC on TCP port 2012.

The issue, tracked as CVE-2026-123890 , combines a Secure Remote Password (SRP) session-key getter that is callable before authentication with an RPC context lookup that does not enforce ownership. The context handles use predictable UUIDv1 values. By creating two reference contexts around a pending login, an attacker can narrow the hidden login handle to a short timestamp window and ask the server for its key.

We reproduced the bypass against vCenter Server Appliance 8.0.2.00400, build 23929136 , running Photon OS 4.0, and leveraged the resulting administrator connection through to root command execution. SSH and Bash access were initially disabled. The attack recovered the directory database and its Security Token Service (STS) signing key, forged an administrator API session, and used appliance administration features to create an account and enable SSH. Neither the existing SSO password nor the root password was an input to that chain.

The attacker needs network access to TCP 2012 and the name of an existing SSO principal. Our PoC uses the default SSO administrator principal, [email protected] ; deployments using a different SSO domain need the corresponding account name. The attack does not depend on an existing authenticated connection or an administrator being online. The attacker initiates the login attempt in order to recover its session key.

The authenticated connection can call the directory database-copy interface. Its raw database contains the STS private signing key, allowing the attacker to sign administrator assertions that vCenter trusts. Our attack workflow used a forged assertion to create a local appliance superAdmin account, enable SSH and Bash, and execute commands as root through sudo. This final part requires access to the HTTPS API on TCP 443 and SSH on TCP 22, in addition to TCP 2012. It uses the administrative privileges obtained through the original bypass; no second vulnerability is needed once you have administrative RPC access.

The SRP RPC getter receives an SRP verifier at [A1], obtains its session key at [A2], and returns a copy at [A3]. It checks whether the verifier and key exist. It does not check whether the caller has authenticated, whether the caller owns the verifier, or whether the SRP client has supplied a valid password proof.

The value at [A1] is a server-side pointer that the RPC runtime resolves from the context handle supplied in the request. Each newly created verifier context receives its own UUID, so the attacker needs the handle for the specific pending login they want to complete. These context UUIDs identify individual verifier instances; the fixed interface UUIDs identify the available RPC interfaces.

UUIDv1 values contain a timestamp, clock sequence, and node identifier. The attacker creates a verifier immediately before starting the target login and another immediately afterward, obtaining both reference UUIDs without authenticating. On the tested service, the reference UUIDs share their clock sequence and node identifier. The attacker reuses those fields and searches the intervening timestamps for the pending login's live verifier, keeping the login connection open throughout the search.

The underlying SRP library exposes the authentication flag separately at [A4]. Its key getter at [A5] does not consult that flag. The server has already computed the shared key while generating its challenge, because it needs that key to verify the client's proof. Returning it at this stage gives the client the secret needed to produce that proof.

The RPC runtime takes the supplied UUID at [B1], searches a process-wide table at [B2], and returns the associated context at [B3]. There is no comparison with the requesting connection or its authenticated identity. Although context creation records a client for lifetime management, that client is not part of this lookup's authorization decision (hooray!!).

We confirmed the boundary directly: one anonymous RPC connection created an SRP verifier, and a second anonymous connection retrieved that verifier's key before any password proof was sent. The first connection's context handle was enough to make the second connection's request succeed.

The service registers the helper with a secure-interface flag and an authentication callback. On the tested TCP path, however, the RPC executor retrieves those registration values and dispatches the server stub without enforcing the callback. Other directory handlers perform their own access checks. The SRP key getter has no equivalent check, leaving it reachable before authentication.

The UUID timestamp generator reads the wall clock at [C1] and converts its microseconds into 100-nanosecond UUID ticks at [C2]. The resulting UUIDv1 handle also contains a clock sequence and node identifier. In our captures, those latter fields remain stable while newly created contexts advance through the timestamp space.

vmdir manages this SRP authentication exchange through the Generic Security Service API (GSS-API), which provides a common interface for authentication mechanisms. Starting the login creates an SRP verifier internally, but the login client does not receive its context handle.

An attacker can obtain reference handles by creating ordinary SRP verifiers through the helper interface. Creating one immediately before the GSS-API login and another immediately afterward bounds the timestamp of its hidden verifier. The shared node and clock-sequence fields then let the attacker construct candidate UUIDs within that interval.

The default search advances in increments of ten UUID ticks, matching the clock's microsecond resolution. The generator also supports uniqueness adjustments within a clock tick, so the PoC includes a one-tick search mode. Clock changes, concurrent activity, and the chosen search bounds can affect recovery; exhausting the window does not establish that a target is patched.

The service exposes two relevant RPC interfaces on port 2012:
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text

### Hackers Found a Way Around GitHub SHA Pinning. This Webinar Explains How to Close the Gap
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-24T12:42:09Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: supply_chain
- Summary: Pinning GitHub Actions to an exact version of their code, known as SHA pinning, has become the go-to defense against software supply chain attacks. Attackers have already found a way around it. In March 2026, the hackers who hijacked Trivy, a widely used open-source security scanner, planted malicious code that looked like it came from GitHub's official checkout tool. They even labeled it a
- Technical details: GitHub Actions attackers exploited SHA pinning trust gaps to disguise malicious commits, while new workflow lockfiles leave provenance risks unresolve

Pinning GitHub Actions to an exact version of their code, known as SHA pinning, has become the go-to defense against software supply chain attacks. Attackers have already found a way around it.

In March 2026, the hackers who hijacked Trivy, a widely used open-source security scanner, planted malicious code that looked like it came from GitHub's official checkout tool. They even labeled it a security fix. Teams pinned to it would have passed every check.

The trick works because GitHub lets copies of a project, called forks, share code with the original. In May, the same technique hit the popular actions-cool/issues-helper tool, stealing credentials from the pipelines that ran it.

The lesson: pinning guarantees the code won't change, but not who wrote it.

That gap is the focus of an upcoming webinar, GitHub Actions Security Beyond SHA Pinning . If your team treats pinning as the finish line, save your seat to learn what it actually protects you from, and what it doesn't.

Pinning earned its reputation. When the tj-actions/changed-files compromise repointed tags to a malicious commit across more than 23,000 repositories in March 2025, teams that had pinned to SHAs were unaffected.

GitHub now lets administrators enforce pinning as an organization-wide policy, and few security teams argue against it.

📅 Save Your Spot: Learn Why “Best Practice” Won’t Stop the Next GitHub Actions Attack

Authorship: A SHA doesn't tell you who wrote the commit. Any fork commit can be pinned.

Project membership: Org-level enforcement checks that a reference is a 40-character SHA, not that the commit actually lives in the upstream project.

Runtime: A Docker-based action pinned to a SHA can still build its image at runtime, pulling in dependencies that were never frozen.

With agentic development pushing more code through CI than ever, each of those gaps gets exercised more often and reviewed less closely.

GitHub's 2026 Actions security roadmap, published March 26, put deterministic dependency locking front and center. Workflow lockfiles entered public preview on October 14 and became generally available on October 28, 2026. They tie every run to recorded commits for actions and their transitive dependencies, even if a tag moves later.

That's real progress. Locked commits must exist on a branch of the source repository, which blunts fork-only impostors. But GitHub's own changelog is clear that locking "does not determine whether the dependency itself is safe." Commit authorship and runtime builds remain open; immutable releases only help when maintainers opt in, and lockfiles aren't currently supported on GitHub Enterprise Server.

Not sure where your pipelines fall in that gap? Register for the webinar for a clear-eyed breakdown of what GitHub's roadmap fixes and what it still leaves to you.

Led by Erika Heidi , Staff Developer Relations Engineer at Chainguard , the session draws a hard line between immutability and provenance, and covers:

What org-level pin enforcement actually verifies, and the false confidence it can create

How impostor commits work, and why GitHub's repository network makes them possible
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text
- EPSS context: EPSS unavailable / not applicable — FIRST.org; not an asset-specific risk score
- [Open source item](https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/hackers-found-way-around-github-sha.html)

A Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) base score, which gives a detailed severity rating, is available for each vulnerability from the CVE link(s) in the References section.

Red Hat Product Security has rated this update as having a security impact of Important. A Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) base score, which gives a detailed severity rating, is available for each vulnerability from the CVE link(s) in the References section.

The SMB/CIFS protocol is a standard file sharing protocol widely deployed on Microsoft Windows machines. The cifs-utils packages contain tools for mounting shares on Linux using the SMB/CIFS protocol. The tools in this package work in conjunction with support in the kernel to allow one to mount a SMB/CIFS share onto a client and use it as if it were a standard Linux file system.

cifs-utils: mount.cifs credential-file option injection allows local privilege boundary bypass (CVE-2026-104715)

For more details about the security issue(s), including the impact, a CVSS score, acknowledgments, and other related information, refer to the CVE page(s) listed in the References section.

### Google Researcher Shows How a Single Bit Change in .NET Files Bypassed Windows Code Integrity
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-24T12:02:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-108398, CVE-2026-87268 | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: A quirk in how the .NET Framework loads a program file let a local attacker run untrusted code past two separate protections on Windows built to block it, Google Project Zero researcher James Forshaw showed in a write-up published yesterday. The same loading behavior bypassed Oracle VirtualBox's process hardening on Windows hosts, tracked as CVE-2026-87268, and Windows code integrity, tracked
- Technical details: Google Project Zero details how .NET Framework loading behavior enabled local security bypasses in VirtualBox and Windows, both patched.

A quirk in how the .NET Framework loads a program file let a local attacker run untrusted code past two separate protections on Windows built to block it, Google Project Zero researcher James Forshaw showed in a write-up published yesterday.

The same loading behavior bypassed Oracle VirtualBox's process hardening on Windows hosts, tracked as CVE-2026-87268 , and Windows code integrity, tracked as CVE-2026-108398 .

Oracle fixed the VirtualBox flaw in September, and Microsoft fixed the Windows one on October 13, both before the write-up appeared.

VirtualBox on Windows monitors the system calls that load program files into its virtual machine processes, so a less-trusted user on the same computer cannot push code into them. Forshaw got past that watch through the .NET Framework.

The framework can run a .NET program file from a plain copy in memory when the normal way of loading it, as an executable image, fails. Version 4 of the framework takes that fallback on one specific error, and the memory copy skips the image checks a loaded program would face.

The VirtualBox check returned that error for any .NET file compiled as AnyCPU, a form meant to run on any processor, so an unchanged file loaded through the path the hardening never inspects.

To get the file loaded while a virtual machine was started, he registered a COM object in the current user's settings that a protected VirtualBox process would pull in, a method known as COM hijacking . He set out the mechanism in his technical analysis .

Oracle rates the flaw, CVE-2026-87268, as a local problem that needs low privileges and no user interaction, scores it 7.8, and lists VirtualBox 7.2.16 on Windows hosts as affected. Its security advisory says a successful attack can result in the "takeover of Oracle VM VirtualBox."

The code runs inside the protected VirtualBox process. It is not a break out of a guest virtual machine onto the host, and the attacker begins as an ordinary user on that host.

The same idea worked against Windows' own code-integrity checks, which decide what user-mode code is allowed to run. There is no VirtualBox hook here, only the system call that maps a file as an image.

The researcher found that clearing a single bit in a .NET file's header, the IMAGE_FILE_EXECUTABLE_IMAGE flag, made that call reject the file while .NET still loaded and ran it.

"This single bit change is all you need to load an untrusted .NET assembly," he wrote. He tried it on Windows 11 S Mode, which enables User Mode Code Integrity (UMCI), the policy that limits which user-mode programs can run. A logic error in .NET's own policy check let the file load even with UMCI enforced.

The flaw, CVE-2026-108398, works through the .NET Framework version 4 loading path and needs a program willing to load the assembly. It is not a way to seize any Windows machine over the internet.

Microsoft rates it a local, low-privilege security-feature bypass, scores it 8.8, and fixed it on October 13. The company lists it as publicly disclosed but not used in attacks.

The same file reached a more sensitive target as well. Protected Process Light, or PPL, is a kind of Windows process that ordinary programs, even ones with administrator rights, are not meant to enter. By hijacking a COM object that WerSecureFault.exe loads, he ran his test code inside a PPL process at the highest protection level without administrator rights.

The older Protected Process type resisted the technique because the .NET runtime files lack the page hashes it requires. The October fix corrects the policy check for UMCI but does not change loading for protected processes, so that PPL route should still work, according to the researcher. It carries no separate CVE.

On Windows hosts, update VirtualBox to a build that carries the September fixes. The affected versions are 7.2.16 and 7.2.18, and 7.2.18 shipped on September 15 with that set. An upstream code fix is not the same as a released binary, so check the running version rather than assume an older build already has it.

.NET systems need the October 13 update that matches the exact Windows and .NET Framework version in use, not one shared package. The update release notes list the right KB for each. On Windows 11 version 26H2, for example, it is KB5127694.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text

### 2027 SOC Budget Reset: 5 Priorities to Cut Costs and Reduce Cyber Risk
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-24T12:01:54Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: What if your biggest SOC budget problem isn't how much you spend, but how much analyst time you lose? According to ANY.RUN's estimates, SOC teams could recover 450 analyst hours per 1,000 investigated alerts through faster threat analysis and IOC research. That's a significant opportunity as manual investigations, repeated checks, and unnecessary escalations continue to drain resources. As
- Technical details: 2027 SOC Budget Reset: 5 Priorities to Cut Costs and Reduce Cyber Risk | Read more hacking news on The Hacker News cybersecurity news website and learn how to protect against cyberattacks and software vulnerabilities.

What if your biggest SOC budget problem isn't how much you spend, but how much analyst time you lose?

According to ANY.RUN 's estimates, SOC teams could recover 450 analyst hours per 1,000 investigated alerts through faster threat analysis and IOC research. That's a significant opportunity as manual investigations, repeated checks, and unnecessary escalations continue to drain resources.

As 2027 approaches, here are five priorities to reduce SOC costs, recover capacity, and strengthen protection against costly cyberattacks.

Cybersecurity spending is facing conflicting pressures. According to IANS and Artico Search, 55% of CISOs reported flat or declining budgets in 2026 . Meanwhile, PwC's 2027 Global Digital Trust Insights found that 84% of surveyed security and finance leaders expect cybersecurity budgets to increase .

Growing workloads: More alerts and complex threats demand additional investigation capacity.

Rising operational costs: Manual processes, staffing needs, and expanding security infrastructure put pressure on budgets.

Pressure to prove ROI: Every investment must demonstrate measurable improvements in security and operational efficiency.

Increasing cyber risks: Cost reductions cannot come at the expense of detection coverage or response capabilities.

AI is becoming a major cybersecurity investment priority, but adding AI capabilities won't automatically improve SOC efficiency. For 2027, the focus should be on reducing investigation costs, improving analyst productivity, and avoiding unnecessary escalations.

For example, ANY.RUN's Interactive Sandbox combines automated threat analysis with AI-powered reporting to help teams achieve these goals:

Cut manual investigation time: Automated Interactivity follows redirects, extracts QR code links, and handles supported CAPTCHAs, exposing malicious activity that would otherwise require additional analyst effort.

Reduce investigation and escalation costs: AI-powered Tier 1 reports provide clear summaries of malicious behavior and recommended next steps. This helps analysts make faster decisions and reduces the need for repeated investigations and unnecessary Tier 2 involvement.

These improvements have measurable business value. ANY.RUN reports up to 20% lower Tier 1 workload and 30% fewer Tier 1-to-Tier 2 escalations , helping SOCs handle growing workloads with less pressure to expand their teams.

The 2027 budget priority: Evaluate AI investments based on investigation time, workload reduction, and escalation rates, not the number of AI features included.

Reduce Tier 1 workload by up to 20% and control investigation costs.

As alert volumes grow, hiring additional analysts may seem like the obvious solution. But before expanding headcount, SOCs should calculate how much capacity they could recover by improving investigation efficiency.

ANY.RUN estimates that faster behavioral analysis through its Interactive Sandbox, combined with less manual threat research, can save 450 analyst hours per 1,000 investigated alerts .

Illustrative calculation based on ANY.RUN's estimate and an assumed hourly rate. Actual results depend on SOC workloads and investigation processes.

Binding Operational Directive (BOD) 26-04: Prioritizing Security Updates Based on Risk establishes vulnerability management requirements for Federal Civilian Executive Branch (FCEB) agencies. BOD 26-04 reinforces the importance of the KEV Catalog and requires federal agencies to prioritize rapid remediation of high-risk vulnerabilities, specifically those identified by Comm
- Technical details: CISA has added three new vulnerabilities to its KEV Catalog, based on evidence of active exploitation.

Official websites use .gov A .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPS A lock ( ) or https:// means you’ve safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

CISA has added three new vulnerabilities to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) Catalog , based on evidence of active exploitation.

CVE-2026-131914 Idunno AntiCsrf Deserialization of Untrusted Data Vulnerability

CVE-2026-132165 Drupal Core Remote Code Execution Vulnerability

CVE-2026-132224 Optimizely Web Experimentation External Control of Critical State Data Vulnerability

These types of vulnerabilities are a frequent attack vector for malicious cyber actors and pose significant risks to the federal enterprise.

Binding Operational Directive (BOD) 26-04: Prioritizing Security Updates Based on Risk establishes vulnerability management requirements for Federal Civilian Executive Branch (FCEB) agencies. BOD 26-04 reinforces the importance of the KEV Catalog and requires federal agencies to prioritize rapid remediation of high-risk vulnerabilities, specifically those identified by Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVEs) listed in CISA’s KEV Catalog on publicly exposed assets that grant total control of the asset post-exploitation, while deferring action for lower-risk vulnerabilities. BOD 26-04 further establishes basic expectations for when agencies must check whether threat actors compromised the system before the patch was applied.

While BOD 26-04 applies only to FCEB agencies, CISA encourages all organizations to adopt risk-based vulnerability management and prioritize remediation of KEV Catalog vulnerabilities . CISA will continue to add vulnerabilities to the catalog that meet the specified criteria .

Aware of an exploited vulnerability not currently listed in the KEV Catalog? Submit it for potential addition through CISA’s KEV Nomination Form . Potential KEV additions must have a CVE ID, evidence of exploitation, and clear mitigation guidance.

This product is provided subject to this Notification and this Privacy & Use policy.

We recently updated our anonymous product survey ; we welcome your feedback.

- Technical details: View CSAF Summary Successful exploitation of this vulnerability could allow an attacker to execute arbitrary code with webserver privileges. The following versions of Danfoss AK-SM 8xxA Series are affected: AK-SM8xxA <=5.4.0 (CVE-2026-129713) CVSS Vendor Equipment v3 9.8 Danfoss AK-SM8xxA 1 Vulnerability Stack-based Buffer Overflow Background Critical Infrastructure Sectors: Commercial Facilities Countries/Areas Deployed: Worldwide Company Headquarters Location: Denmark Vulnerabilities CVE-2026-129713 A stack-based buffer overflow in an unauthenticated CGI handler of the Danfoss AK-SM 8xxA Series controller allows remote attackers to send specially crafted HTTP/S POST requests, leading to memory corruption, webserver restart and hypothetically potential arbitrary code execution with webserver privileges. Authentication is not required to exploit this vulnerability. Read More 1 Affected Product Danfoss AK-SM8xxA <=5.4.0 Product Status: known_affected Remediations Mitigation This vulnerability will be addressed in an upcoming Danfoss AK-SM 800A Series software release (version 5.5.x). In addition, Danfoss will remove the ability for users to disable Strict Mode in future software releases. Mitigation Until a software update is available, Danfoss recommends the following mitigations: * Follow the guidance provided in the product documentation and do not expose devices directly to the Internet or assign them public IP addresses. * Restrict remote access to affected devices by using a secure VPN or other protected network architecture. * Configure all affected devices to operate in Strict Mode, which provides enhanced security protections. * Use strong, unique passwords for all user accounts and administrative access. * For additional security recommendations and best practices see the Danfoss AK-SM 800 security guidance page: https://www.danfoss.com/en/service-and-support/downloads/dcs/adap-kool-software/ak-sm-800a/#tab-overview Additional Metrics Relevant CWE: CWE-121 Stack-based Buffer Overflow CVSS Version Base Score Base Severity Vector String 3.1 9.8 CRITICAL CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:H/I:H/A:H 4.0 9.3 CRITICAL CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:N/PR:N/UI:N/VC:H/VI:H/VA:H/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N View CVE Details Acknowledgments Carlo Meijer of Midnight Blue reported CVE-2026-129713 to CISA. Recommended Practices CISA recommends users take defensive measures to minimize the risk of exploitation of these vulnerabilities. Minimize network exposure for all control system devices and/or systems, ensuring they are not accessible from the internet . Locate control system networks and remote devices behind firewalls and isolating them from business networks. When remote access is required, use more secure methods, such as Virtual Private Networks (VPNs), recognizing VPNs may have vulnerabilities and should be updated to the most current version available. Also recognize VPN is only as secure as the connected devices. CISA reminds organizations to perform proper impact analysis and risk assessment prior to deploying defensive measures. CISA also provides a section for control systems security recommended practices on the ICS webpage on cisa.gov/ics . Several CISA products detailing cyber defense best practices are available for reading and download, including Improving Industrial Control Systems Cybersecurity with Defense-in-Depth Strategies . CISA encourages organizations to implement recommended cybersecurity strategies for proactive defense of ICS assets . Additional mitigation guidance and recommended practices are publicly available on the ICS webpage at cisa.gov/ics in the technical information paper, ICS-TIP-12-146-01B--Targeted Cyber Intrusion Detection and Mitigation Strategies . Organizations observing suspected malicious activity should follow established internal procedures and report findings to CISA for tracking and correlation against other incidents. Schneider Electric is aware of a vulnerability in its EcoStruxure™ Power Build Rapsody product. The EcoStruxure™ Power Build Rapsody is used to enter or import the single line diagram, to get the extensive bill of material of your switchboard, including all devices, connection items, and mounting components. Failure to apply the remediations provided below may risk memory corruption, heap-based buffer overflow, and/or stack-based buffer overflow, which could result in local attackers being able to exploit these issues to potentially execute arbitrary code.

The following versions of Schneider Electric EcoStruxure Power Build Rapsody software are affected:

EcoStruxure Power Build Rapsody software vers:generic/<=2.9.1.0_FR, 2.9.12.0101_FR, vers:generic/<=2.8.5.0301_ESP, 2.9.5.0000_ESP, vers:generic/<=2.8.7.0101_PT, 2.9.7.0000_PT, vers:generic/<=2.8.8.0201_BEL(FR), 2.9.8.0000_BELFR, vers:generic/<=2.8.3.0201_BEL(EN), 2.9.3.0000_BELEN, vers:generic/<=2.8.4.0401_INT(EN), 2.9.4.0000_INTEN, vers:generic/<=2.8.6.0000_INT(FR), 2.9.6.0000_INTFR, vers:generic/<=2.8.2.0000_NL, 2.9.6.0000_NL (CVE-2026-119055, CVE-2026-119055, CVE-2026-119055, CVE-2026-119055, CVE-2026-119055, CVE-2026-119055, CVE-2026-119055, CVE-2026-119055)

Critical Infrastructure Sectors: Energy, Critical Manufacturing, Commercial Facilities

Vendor fix Versions FR V2.9.12.0101, ESP V2.9.5.0000, PT V2.9.7.0000, BEL(FR) V2.9.8.0000, BEL(EN) V2.9.3.0000, INT(EN) V2.9.4.0000, INT(FR)V2.9.6.0000, NL V2.9.6.0000 of EcoStruxure™ Power Build Rapsody software includes a fix for this vulnerability and is available for download here: https://www.se.com/ww/en/product-country-selector/?pageType=product-range&sourceId=2309

Schneider Electric CPCERT reported this vulnerability to CISA.

CISA recommends users take defensive measures to minimize the risk of exploitation of these vulnerabilities.

Minimize network exposure for all control system devices and/or systems, ensuring they are not accessible from the internet .

Locate control system networks and remote devices behind firewalls and isolating them from business networks.

When remote access is required, use more secure methods, such as Virtual Private Networks (VPNs), recognizing VPNs may have vulnerabilities and should be updated to the most current version available. Also recognize VPN is only as secure as the connected devices.

CISA reminds organizations to perform proper impact analysis and risk assessment prior to deploying defensive measures.

CISA also provides a section for control systems security recommended practices on the ICS webpage on cisa.gov/ics . Several CISA products detailing cyber defense best practices are available for reading and download, including Improving Industrial Control Systems Cybersecurity with Defense-in-Depth Strategies .

CISA encourages organizations to implement recommended cybersecurity strategies for proactive defense of ICS assets .

Additional mitigation guidance and recommended practices are publicly available on the ICS webpage at cisa.gov/ics in the technical information paper, ICS-TIP-12-146-01B--Targeted Cyber Intrusion Detection and Mitigation Strategies .

Organizations observing suspected malicious activity should follow established internal procedures and report findings to CISA for tracking and correlation against other incidents.

This is republished to CISA's website as a means of increasing visibility and is provided "as-is" for informational purposes only. CISA is not responsible for the editorial or technical accuracy of republished advisories and provides no warranties of any kind regarding any information contained within this advisory. - Technical details: Official websites use .gov A .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Successful exploitation of this vulnerability could allow attackers to corrupt application memory and cause instability or a crash, potentially leading to arbitrary code execution, if a user is convinced to open a malicious file.

The following versions of AutomationDirect C-More EA9 Programming Software are affected:

C-More EA9 Programming Software <=V6.80 (CVE-2026-127280)

Critical Infrastructure Sectors: Critical Manufacturing, Energy, Water and Wastewater Systems

Vendor fix Update C-More EA9 Programming Software to V6.81: https://www.automationdirect.com/support/software-downloads

Mitigation If you cannot update to the latest software or firmware version right away, apply the following compensating controls to reduce risk until the update can be performed. These measures are temporary and do not replace the update, which remains the primary and recommended remediation. Apply these controls as appropriate based on the vulnerability, assessed risk, operational requirements, and characteristics of your environment. Implement changes under your established management-of-change process. Before implementing isolation, access-control, or network-configuration changes, evaluate potential impacts to safety, availability, and essential operational functions. • Isolate affected engineering workstations and OT systems from external and nonessential networks, including the internet and corporate IT networks, where operationally feasible. • Disable or tightly restrict vendor, contractor, and other remote access to the affected OT environment until the vulnerability can be remediated. Where remote access is operationally required, use a hardened jump host, enforce multi-factor authentication, apply least-privilege access, and limit access to authorized, time-bound sessions. • Use trusted, dedicated OT networks for device communication. Where practicable and appropriate to the assessed risk, use dedicated or physically separated infrastructure for higher-criticality systems. Where logical segmentation is used, ensure segmentation controls are appropriately configured, secured, and monitored. • Restrict both physical and logical access to affected systems to authorized personnel only. Apply least-privilege principles to administrative, engineering, maintenance, and service access. • Configure application allowlisting, where supported, so that only trusted and pre-approved applications are permitted to execute. Block unauthorized software. • Use appropriate antivirus or endpoint detection and response (EDR) tools where supported and compatible with the affected system. Configure host-based and network firewalls to permit only required communications. • Disable unnecessary services, ports, protocols, and communication pathways associated with the affected systems. Where a service or protocol cannot be disabled for operational reasons, restrict access to trusted systems and authorized users wherever possible. • Enable and regularly review available system, security, and network logs to identify suspicious or unauthorized activity. Where appropriate, monitor communications to and from affected systems for unexpected connections or changes in network behavior. • Maintain secure, tested, and preferably offline backups of PLC programs, device configurations, engineering workstation configurations, and other information necessary to restore operations. Periodically verify that backups can be successfully restored. • Verify that critical OT functions can continue operating without unnecessary dependencies on external networks, cloud services, remote-access services, corporate IT services, or other nonessential systems. • If removable media is required to transfer files into an isolated or restricted environment, use controlled and trusted media and scan it for malicious content before use. • Continuously evaluate the risks associated with operating vulnerable or outdated firmware or software. Adjust compensating controls as the threat environment, operational requirements, or available vulnerability information changes. These compensating controls are intended to reduce exposure while remediation is pending. Organizations should prioritize applying the vendor-provided update as soon as it can be safely tested and deployed within the operational environment.

### Introducing CORNERSTONE: delivering external services for the NCSC
- Source: UK NCSC | Published: 2026-11-24T12:00:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: The NCSC is seeking industry feedback on a new delivery model for modernising external cyber security services.
- Technical details: The NCSC is seeking industry feedback on a new delivery model for modernising external cyber security services.

The NCSC's existing services have developed strong standards, trusted market relationships and significant reach. But as demand grows, we need to consider how these services can be delivered in ways that are scalable, sustainable, and responsive to the UK’s changing needs.

As a result, the NCSC is exploring how it can modernise the way its external services are designed, delivered and developed. We want to create an operating model that enables NCSC services to achieve greater impact, reach more organisations, respond more quickly to changing threats and technology, and make better use of the capabilities that exist across the cyber security industry.

CORNERSTONE is a multi-year project to develop a more scalable relationship between the NCSC and industry, with each focusing on where it can add most value. Our ambition for CORNERSTONE is not simply to outsource services currently run by the NCSC; we want to make our services more impactful, accessible and adaptable. This will allow the NCSC to focus on those areas where it brings unique insight as the national technical authority for cyber security.

We want organisations with cyber security expertise and real-world experience of assurance and service delivery to challenge our thinking before any future competition. More specifically, we are seeking feedback on a proposal which sees industry partners delivering the following NCSC services:

Currently, CHECK and CRTF are distinct assurance schemes operated with significant NCSC involvement, while the existing PDNS contract is a different kind of service altogether. The proposed new PDNS assurance scheme would create something new rather than simply transfer the existing PDNS service into the arrangement.

The purpose of bringing these areas together is to explore whether there are common administrative, data and operating capabilities that can be provided more effectively. These could include:

providing common tools and infrastructure where appropriate

Meaningful market engagement depends on hearing from organisations with real-world experience of cyber security assurance and operating at scale. We therefore encourage you to read the accompanying Market Engagement Prospectus (PDF) , and provide feedback on the commercial and practical viability of our assumptions. These include:

this phase of CORNERSTONE will operate as a self-funding concession model

the NCSC will remain the root of trust for badging and assuring schemes and services

the NCSC will remain the owner of the NCSC-assured brand

organisations will work collaboratively with the NCSC to bring together expertise to deliver innovative new schemes and services

delivery partners will work in accordance with our security requirements for managing and storing data

delivery partners will ensure services are able to respond at pace to the changing technological and geopolitical environment

We’re hosting two identical Supplier Information Sessions over MS Teams on 2nd December and 9th December, 10:00 – 11:00. During these meetings, we will talk through our plans and provide an opportunity for interested organisations to hear more about CORNERSTONE. Questions and answers from these sessions will be captured and made available to all interested parties. You can register your place by clicking on one of the dates below:

We anticipate beginning the tender process in Spring 2027 and are considering a contract length of around five years. However, our longer-term ambition for CORNERSTONE is broader than the initial three services described above. We intend to explore a scalable operating model through which the NCSC can govern, assure and enable a wider portfolio of external services, including both cyber assurance and Active Cyber Defence services, where industry delivery is appropriate.

In the meantime, we welcome thoughts, comments and questions by 18th December at [email protected] .

### Familiar weaknesses, exploited faster: defending against agentic cyber attacks
- Source: UK NCSC | Published: 2026-11-24T12:00:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: ai_threat
- Summary: How to protect your organisation from cyber attacks created using ‘agentic offensive frameworks’.
- Technical details: How to protect your organisation from cyber attacks created using ‘agentic offensive frameworks’.

Since September 2026, the NCSC has seen a marked rise in AI-assisted cyber attacks. Organisations across multiple sectors are reporting incidents as a result of threat actors using ‘agentic offensive frameworks'; software originally designed for red teaming being applied beyond its defensive purpose.

The incidents involved automated identification and exploitation attempts across organisations' internet-facing services and assets in quick succession. This resulted in downtime to some services or the compromise of systems, providing attackers with a foothold for further agent-driven activity (or in some cases, human-driven activity within their network).

none of the weaknesses being exploited are new or novel

the tools the agents have access to are common and provide no new capability the typical threat actor would have access to

What has changed, is how quickly and persistently threat actors can conduct attacks – at low cost – using these frameworks combined with cheap, capable open models or illegal API resellers.

This blog explains how agentic offensive frameworks are being used, what this means for cyber defenders, and the steps you should take to help protect your organisation.

An agentic offensive framework allows AI models to interact with a set of off-the-shelf cyber security tools for the purpose of probing digital systems for weaknesses. Typically, a framework will:

At the time of writing (November 2026), this type of activity has become synonymous with the ARTEX framework, a multi-agent penetration testing and red-teaming tool originally developed for authorised security testing. Different tools come and go (others include Cairn, PentAGI, RedAmon, Xalgorix and Darkmoon), so rather than concentrating on a particular framework, here we’ll focus on how the combination of a human attacker plus a capable model plus a framework creates an uplift in capability.

Compared with a human attacker working without tools, an agent plus a framework:

works quickly (it moves from discovery to exploitation in minutes)

is exhaustive in its search (it will try every endpoint, parameter and credential it finds)

instantly learns from failures (it creates memory, reads error messages, responds and adapts its next attempt)

is cheap to run, at scale (the cost of running automated attacks is low)

This does mean that today's frameworks are noisy. They trade stealth for speed, generate a lot of traffic and will interact with almost anything they find, which makes them easier to detect . This may not always be the case if future frameworks prioritise stealth.

Notably, the issues being exploited currently are not novel memory corruption vulnerabilities (or even known but unpatched vulnerabilities), but unmitigated weaknesses that are often the result of years of technical debt that organisations have accumulated. Agentic attacks are exploiting low hanging fruit such as:

Once an initial foothold has been gained, the agents explore the environment, exploiting the fact that many credentials in an organisation are over-privileged for their needs, and networks are often flat or interconnected.

Organisations need strong operational discipline to avoid being overwhelmed by the volume, intensity and duration of attacks from agentic offensive frameworks. Answering the following questions will help you develop a holistic approach that can protect your organisation.

- Technical details: An update for libreoffice is now available for Red Hat Enterprise Linux 9.4 Update Services for SAP Solutions.

Red Hat Product Security has rated this update as having a security impact of Important. A Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) base score, which gives a detailed severity rating, is available for each vulnerability from the CVE link(s) in the References section.

LibreOffice is an open source, community-developed office productivity suite. It includes key desktop applications, such as a word processor, a spreadsheet, a presentation manager, a formula editor, and a drawing program. LibreOffice replaces OpenOffice and provides a similar but enhanced and extended office suite.

libreoffice: heap buffer overflow in PDF import stream handling (CVE-2026-63274)

libreoffice: libreoffice: Arbitrary code execution via remote Java database driver (CVE-2026-63277)

### Unclassified intel system hit by hackers, affecting CIA, ODNI
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: CyberScoop | Published: 2026-11-24T22:01:23Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: CyberScoop confirmed the incident, with a source saying “no other systems are currently at risk.” The post Unclassified intel system hit by hackers, affecting CIA, ODNI appeared first on CyberScoop .
- Business background / implications: The CIA and Office of the Director of National Intelligence confirmed a data breach affecting an unclassified open-source intelligence system.

The CIA and Office of the Director of National Intelligence have discovered a data breach of an open-source intelligence analysis system, CyberScoop has confirmed.

The New York Times first reported the breach Tuesday, writing that the breach had exposed some unclassified information. A source familiar with the breach said there was a disruption, but the exposure was limited.

‘The hack did not involve our classified networks or classified information, and no other systems are currently at risk,” the source said.

The discovery is the latest in a series of intrusions affecting U.S. security agencies, including two at the FBI and another at the National Security Agency .

Ben Bernstein, manager of cybersecurity advisers at Huntress, said in an emailed comment that while the reported breach didn’t hit classified data, open-source (OSINT) information can still be sensitive.

“Calling this ‘unclassified’ doesn’t make it harmless,” he said. “These systems pull in public and commercial info like news, satellite imagery, and paid data feeds, so the raw material isn’t a state secret. The value for an adversary is seeing what the CIA and ODNI are doing with it.”

“If you can see what analysts are searching for and what topics they’re tracking, you get a solid look at U.S. intelligence priorities and blind spots without ever touching a classified file,” he continued. “These OSINT networks are also tough to secure because they have to stay open to the public internet to do their job.”

The CIA declined to comment and the Office of the Director of National Intelligence did not immediately respond to a request for comment Tuesday.

### 'God Mode' Flaws Could Wreak Border-Control Havoc
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: Dark Reading | Published: 2026-11-24T21:36:11Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: Attackers could have disrupted traveler entry into Nigeria, Ethiopia, Zambia, Sri Lanka, Fiji, and Ghana via several vulnerabilities in their online visa and immigration systems.
- Business background / implications: Attackers could have disrupted entry into Nigeria, Ethiopia, Zambia, Sri Lanka, Fiji, and Ghana via vulnerabilities in their online border-control systems

Attackers could have disrupted traveler entry into Nigeria, Ethiopia, Zambia, Sri Lanka, Fiji, and Ghana via several vulnerabilities in their online visa and immigration systems.

Imagine trying to enter a country you've always wanted to visit, only to find yourself not only denied entry but potentially arrested because you've mistakenly ended up on a list of known terrorists.

This is one of several scenarios that could have unfolded if attackers had exploited flaws that independent security researcher Eaton Zveare discovered in the online passport and border-control systems of several countries: Nigeria, Ethiopia, Fiji, Zambia, Sri Lanka, and Ghana.

Though specific to each system, the flaws generally allowed unauthorized access to millions of travelers' sensitive visa and passport data — potentially including biometric information such as fingerprints — and, in some cases, even granted access to administrative functions that could be used to approve or deny entry into a country.

Specifically, Zveare discovered critical API vulnerabilities , including broken object-level authorization (BOLA), broken function-level authorization (BFLA), and missing authentication, in online systems these countries use to process visa applications and authorize travelers to enter.

Related: Navigating Vulnerability Hysteria in the AI Era

Many of the systems were built by third-party contractors with varying levels of expertise, which likely contributed to the relatively simple and easily avoidable security gaps, Zveare tells Dark Reading. In Nigeria's case, an over-reliance on AI to build the systems may also have introduced flaws into the code.

"I think they've gotten on the AI hype train, and they've kind of used it to build their border control a little bit," he says.

Taken together, the vulnerabilities — which should never exist in "the systems that are vital to national security" — highlight a broader failure to secure critical national infrastructure , potentially with significant consequences for people traveling to these countries, Zveare says.

"The tip of the iceberg is not only viewing all the people who ever applied for these visas and passports , but actually being able to approve or deny applications that are currently coming in," he says, describing the potential for bad actors to exploit the flaws.

### Maple raises $5.3M to build AI products that protect your data, even from itself
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: TechCrunch Security | Published: 2026-11-24T21:19:52Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The startup's encrypted AI architecture is designed to prevent even the company itself from accessing users' private data.
- Business background / implications: The startup's encrypted AI architecture is designed to prevent even the company itself from accessing users' private data.

As AI assistants evolve into more capable agents that can access our files, emails, calendar, browser, and more, the amount of trust we’re expected to place in AI companies is growing. One startup called Maple , co-founded by a former Apple engineer who worked on privacy-preserving technologies, is taking a different approach with its AI app, where users’ data remains private — even from the company itself.

“We wanted to build something that was not only encrypted but also could be proven, and that people could go look at the open-source code, and then they could look at what we call trusted execution environments,” explains Maple co-founder and CEO Mark Suman . “They can look at and verify the code that’s running on our servers, so they don’t really have to take our word for it.”

“They can audit everything themselves and know how their data is being handled,” he adds.

The idea of trust is Maple’s larger theme, as the startup proposes that there’s a way to build an AI app where consumers don’t have to trade privacy for convenience, as its competitors often require with their invasive privacy policies and data collection practices , and sometimes even training on users’ personal data .

Instead of asking users to trust that Maple won’t misuse their personal data, it’s attempting to build a business where it can’t access that data in the first place.

Like other AI apps on the market, Maple allows users to engage in a chat experience where they ask questions and get answers. Meanwhile, its newly launched desktop app, Maple Work , offers a more agentic experience where the AI can control your computer, work with your files and apps, and control your browser to get online tasks handled.

With the company’s recently closed $5.3 million round, it’s moving on to its next product — a cloud-based agent that will be able to continue its work even if your computer isn’t on. This addition would make Maple more competitive with other popular consumer AI services, like Meta’s Muse , Instinct , and other AI agents that work over chat.

The oversubscribed seed round was led by Epic Ventures, with participation from Draper Associates, Planetary VC, and Epoch VC, and follows a pre-seed angel round of under a million. Maple plans to raise a Series A in the first half of 2027, given the investor interest.

Suman, who previously spent six years at Appl

### Dozens of oil and shipping tankers hacked in recent weeks, per report
- Category: Cyber incidents & breaches | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: TechCrunch Security | Published: 2026-11-24T21:09:04Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: Iran is the chief suspect behind the dozens of cyberattacks targeting oil and chemical shipping vessels.
- Business background / implications: Iran is the chief suspect behind the dozens of cyberattacks targeting oil and chemical shipping vessels.

U.S. officials suspect Iranian hackers may be behind as many as 50 cyberattacks targeting shipping vessels in recent weeks, according to a report by The Wall Street Journal .

The ships carrying oil, liquefied gas, and other explosive chemicals were hacked as they traveled across Asia and Europe. Several oil tankers headed toward the U.S. coast in August also had their navigation systems breached, prompting cybersecurity teams within the FBI and Coast Guard to board the vessels.

The report said officials are investigating if Iran is expanding its recent wave of hacks against the U.S. by targeting large vessels to sow fear among shippers, disrupt routes, or to cause an explosion at a port. (Ships have backup controls in the event that automated systems are hacked or fail.)

Hackers may have been abusing a bug dubbed FortiBleed , which allows hackers to break into networks by compromising vulnerable Fortinet-made routers. A joint FBI-Coast Guard security alert from September warned that Fortinet routers are used on shipping vessels.

Iran has been firing back on all sides since the start of the U.S./Israel-led war earlier this year, which killed the country’s supreme leader. Many of Iran’s retaliations have been in the form of cyberattacks, with U.S. officials blaming Iran for hacks targeting hundreds of American water providers and U.S. medical tech companies .

A spokesperson for the FBI did not respond to a request for comment about the shipping hacks or whether it had a more accurate figure.

### Colorado says cyberattack took down myColorado app, warns 150,000 had data stolen
- Category: Cyber incidents & breaches | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-24T20:52:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: Cybercriminals likely stole data from 150,000 users of myColorado, which residents typically use for digital identification, document storage and accessing state government services.
- Business background / implications: Cybercriminals likely stole data from 150,000 users of myColorado, which residents typically use for digital identification, document storage and accessing state government services.

A critical app used by thousands of Colorado residents for state services was taken down by a cyberattack last week.

The Governor’s Office of Information Technology (OIT) said the incident was discovered on the evening of November 18 and involved hackers breaching the myColorado app — which Colorado residents use for digital identification, document storage and accessing state government services.

OIT shut down the app and began an investigation. The app is still unavailable as of Tuesday afternoon.

The state noted that the app has 2.1 million users and the cyberattack likely involved the theft of data tied to nearly 150,000 people. Investigators declined to provide more information about what data may have been stolen by the cybercriminals.

“We know how much Coloradans rely on these services, and we understand how this is frustrating and inconvenient. Protecting our users’ data is our first priority, and we will bring systems back online as quickly as we can once security is restored,” said Sarah Tuneberg, executive director of OIT.

Colorado urged residents to bring their physical IDs if they are traveling for the Thanksgiving holiday.

Those seeking to access state services can still do so through Colorado agency websites.

The state previously dealt with major cyberattacks on the Department of Higher Education and ransomware attacks on several smaller municipalities .

Several city and state governments are currently dealing with ransomware attacks and cyber incidents, including agencies in New York, Mississippi and California . On Friday, West Virginia’s legislature was knocked offline due to a cyberattack.

Queens Public Library in New York City confirmed it has nearly recovered from a ransomware attack two weeks ago that shut down services at its 66 branches. The library, which serves the borough’s more than 2 million residents, has been forced to operate without digital services.

The attack was claimed on Sunday by The Gentlemen ransomware gang — a cybercriminal group that claimed 398 attacks last quarter. The group far surpassed all other ransomware gangs with the amount of claimed victims, according to researchers at Halcyon .

In Mississippi, Lauderdale County’s more than 70,000 residents were also impacted by a cyberattack on the sheriff’s office on Monday.

### California privacy agency director Kemp to leave after two-year tenure
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-24T19:30:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The director of the trailblazing California Privacy Protection Agency announced his departure following implementation of a first-in-the-nation platform allowing consumers to easily opt out of data sharing.
- Business background / implications: The director of the trailblazing California Privacy Protection Agency announced his departure following implementation of a first-in-the-nation platform allowing consumers to easily opt out of data sharing.

The director of the trailblazing California Privacy Protection Agency (CalPrivacy) on Tuesday announced his departure after nearly two years leading the organization as it implemented a first-in-the-nation platform allowing consumers to easily opt out of data sharing.

Tom Kemp made the announcement in a LinkedIn post highlighting his work on the so-called Delete Request and Opt-Out Platform (DROP). The system is now used by more than 600,000 Californians who can block data sharing and foil data brokers with the push of a button .

The platform debuted in January, but penalties for companies violating consumers’ data privacy wishes only went into effect in August. Each DROP user has, on average, forced more than 200 deletions by data brokers, Kemp said.

He will begin a new position in January, but did not disclose any details about his new role.

“I am proud that CalPrivacy under my tenure has made great progress in making it significantly easier for Californians to exercise their privacy rights, and that our work represents a case study of how to deliver innovative government services that can make a tangible difference,” he said .

Under Kemp’s stewardship, CalPrivacy also led the push to pass two pieces of legislation expanding consumer privacy, lobbied against federal preemption of California’s tough privacy laws and oversaw the issuance of the largest fine in CalPrivacy’s history.

The agency also adopted the country’s most comprehensive regulations mandating how industry deploys cybersecurity audits, risk assessments and automated decisionmaking technology. The latter refers to software, including algorithms and AI, that processes personal data to make decisions about individuals relating to employment and other significant events.

is a reporter covering digital privacy, surveillance technologies and cybersecurity policy for The Record. She was previously a cybersecurity reporter at CyberScoop. ### UK warns AI-driven cyberattacks are exposing years of weak security
- Category: Cyber incidents & breaches | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: TechCrunch Security | Published: 2026-11-24T18:02:59Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The British cyber agency says that "building strong foundations has never mattered more" as AI hacking tools rely on exploiting simple security flaws.
- Business background / implications: The British cyber agency says that "building strong foundations has never mattered more" as AI hacking tools rely on exploiting simple security flaws.

The U.K. government’s cybersecurity unit says it has seen a “marked rise” in AI-driven cyberattacks in recent months, as hackers increasingly rely on autonomous hacking tools to identify and exploit simple weaknesses to break into systems and networks.

The U.K. National Cybersecurity Center (NCSC) said it has seen several cyber incidents since September involving autonomous hacking tools, such as penetration testing tools used by defenders to stress-test their cybersecurity defenses. These incidents have caused intrusions at organizations and resulted in downtime, the unit said in a blog post .

The NCSC says that while none of the vulnerabilities being exploited are new, the speed at which these AI tools are able to carry out simple hacks has changed.

The rise in AI-related cyberattacks coincides with the broader release earlier this year of open source autonomous penetration testing tools like Artex and Cairn, among others, which anyone can download and use at no cost. The U.S. Federal Bureau of Investigation recently warned that malicious hackers are using AI penetration testing tools to carry out cyberattacks at unprecedented speeds.

While cyberattacks and intrusions are on the rise, the message from London is that many of these attacks are preventable with basic cybersecurity defenses and best practices.

“Notably, the issues being exploited currently are not novel memory corruption vulnerabilities (or even known but unpatched vulnerabilities), but unmitigated weaknesses that are often the result of years of technical debt that organisations have accumulated,” the NCSC wrote.

The unit said that agentic hacking tools often look for exposed or internet-facing systems, then quickly run through lists of weak or easy-to-guess passwords or abuse API endpoints often used by developers to access data.

The NCSC said that AI “does not make established cyber security controls obsolete,” but rather that “building strong foundations has never mattered more.”

The unit added that AI has made it easier to find vulnerabilities and faster to exploit them, warning that weaknesses that have gone unnoticed for years can now be found in minutes.

### Lawmakers warn ID verification companies to bolster security after September hack
- Category: Cyber incidents & breaches | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-24T17:49:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: A bicameral, bipartisan letter from lawmakers said identity verification companies still have significant security work to do in the wake of a data breach at IDscan in September.
- Business background / implications: A bicameral, bipartisan letter from lawmakers said identity verification companies still have significant security work to do in the wake of a data breach at IDscan in September.

A bipartisan group of federal lawmakers on Monday sent a letter focused on security practices to several identity verification companies, citing a September breach that led to scans of more than 150 million people’s drivers’ licenses and other identification documents appearing for sale on the dark web.

The breach of IDScan led to the theft of customer data stored in the company’s cloud platform, spurring an FBI probe and identity theft and fraud concerns for the vast number of Canadian and U.S. victims.

The six lawmakers behind the letter pressured the identity verification firms to provide detailed answers about how they manage customers’ sensitive data and what they are doing to protect it against increasingly common and devastating hacks enabled by artificial intelligence.

They cited not only the risk of fraud and identity theft, but also pointed to the fact that foreign adversaries are increasingly able to “infer Americans’ behavior through commercial data linked to government credentials,” said the letter, signed by Sens. Brian Schatz (D-HI) and Marcia Blackburn (R-TN) with four others from the Senate and House.

“Given the difficulty of tracking these credentials and data sales, remedying this breach will pose substantial challenges even where no further crime is committed.”

Brian Krebs, the independent technology journalist who first reported the IDScan breach in September, said on social media Tuesday that in the past couple of months he has found more than two dozen identity verification companies that store scanned IDs, often by default.

Customers — the companies verifying users’ identities — often have to pay more to avoid the scans from continuing to be collected after verification, Krebs said.

Cybercriminals on the Russia-tied dark web marketplace Nexus began exfiltrating the data more than a year before officials discovered the purloined information online, Krebs reported.

In their letter, the lawmakers directed the identity verification companies to lessen risks by proactively hunting for system vulnerabilities, deploying zero-trust architecture and minimizing threats by limiting collection, sharing and storage of personally identifying information as much as possible.

### Patel mum on FBI crime info database hack, citing ongoing investigation
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: CyberScoop | Published: 2026-11-24T17:48:34Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The breach of the National Crime Information Center was the second in recent months for the bureau. The post Patel mum on FBI crime info database hack, citing ongoing investigation appeared first on CyberScoop .
- Business background / implications: FBI Director Kash Patel declined to share details on the National Crime Information Center hack, citing an active investigation into the breach.

There’s an active investigation into a hack of the FBI’s National Crime Information Center, bureau director Kash Patel said Tuesday, but he otherwise declined to comment on details of the breach.

Patel said “the matter is ongoing” when asked at a new conference about who was behind the hack reported Friday and what information they obtained from the NCIC, a national database for sharing information on warrants, fugitives, missing persons and background checks.

“We’ve publicly released, as we always do, information we are permitted to at this time, but it’s an ongoing investigation,” he said, adding that the FBI was working with other agencies on the matter. “So we’re working with the interagency, and as we’ve done in the past, we will make all the information public either through our partners at the Department of Justice or through our engagements on Capitol Hill.”

President Donald Trump over the weekend said the breach was “not good” and that he’d just learned about it, but otherwise downplayed it, something he often does in response to cyberattacks against the United States.

“I don’t like that,” Trump said. “But maybe we will learn things that we would’ve never learned otherwise.”

It was the second breach in recent months at the FBI, which suffered an attack from ShinyHunters in September that saw the bureau’s website defaced and the hacking group claiming it stole sensitive information on FBI agents and jobseekers.

Patel made his remarks at a Justice Department press conference to tout progress in its battle against scammers — including cybercrime — since Trump signed an executive order in March. As part of his answer to the question about the FBI breach, Patel claimed intrusions were “down 36% based on FBI caseload” under Trump compared to his predecessor, Joe Biden.
