### UK cyber agency, threat companies tracking rise in AI-assisted hacks
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: CyberScoop | Published: 2026-11-24T17:27:09Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The National Cyber Security Centre said it is observing a “marked” increase in such attacks across sectors, as threat actors increasingly delegate to machines. The post UK cyber agency, threat companies tracking rise in AI-assisted hacks appeared first on CyberScoop .
- Business background / implications: The U.K.’s National Cyber Security Centre warns of a marked rise in fast, low-cost AI-assisted cyberattacks using agentic offensive frameworks.

As U.S. policymakers focus on outages that appear partly caused by widespread patching of AI-identified vulnerabilities, the U.K.’s top cyber intelligence agency says it is seeing more cyberattacks carried out with AI assistance.

On Tuesday, three officials from the National Cyber Security Centre said they had observed “a marked rise” in AI-assisted cyberattacks since September.

“Organizations across multiple sectors are reporting incidents as a result of threat actors using ‘agentic offensive frameworks’; software originally designed for red teaming being applied beyond its defensive purpose,” wrote David Chismon, chief technology officer for architecture, and two partially-named NCSC staffers, Kris B., incident handler, and Ellen T, lead data scientist on the vulnerability management team.

The incidents resemble past AI-enabled hacks, defined by rapid, automated vulnerability discovery and attempted exploitation of all of a target’s internet-facing assets “in quick succession.” The impact on victims ranged from outages to system compromise.

“What has changed, is how quickly and persistently threat actors can conduct attacks – at low cost – using these frameworks combined with cheap, capable open models or illegal API resellers,” the officials wrote.

The NCSC officials called the threat sobering but noted that “none” of the vulnerabilities or attacks are novel. They’re known weaknesses that organizations should have patched sooner, and most can still be patched. Additionally, hacking operations moving at speed and scale generate a great deal of “noise” and telemetry that defenders can track.

Large language models are powerful on their own, but combined with technical harnesses , offensive security frameworks , and human expertise, they can become genuine national security threats.

Technical harnesses – systems and tools that can guide and contextualize LLM-driven actions, manage memory and resource requirements, and dictate when human operators need to step in – are particularly valuable to both cybersecurity defenders and malicious hackers.

Eric Loui, a principal intelligence analyst at CrowdStrike, said the company is tracking dozens of threat actor groups that increasingly delegate decisions to agentic AI. Sophisticated harnesses means that even easily accessible open-source LLMs, like Chinese-made Dee
- Action: Not applicable as a vulnerability fix; verify deal stage and material claims against company announcements or filings.

### Carnegie Endowment for International Peace says third-party breach leaked donation info
- Category: Cyber incidents & breaches | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-24T16:00:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: Some donors to the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace had their information leaked due to a cyberattack on a third-party vendor named Soapbox Engage.
- Business background / implications: Some donors to the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace had their information leaked due to a cyberattack on a third-party vendor named Soapbox Engage.

The organization sent emails to past donors on Friday warning of the data breach, writing that the incident occurred on October 28. The threat actors were removed from the vendor’s system on October 30, they said.

Carnegie Endowment for International Peace spokesperson Sharmeen Aly confirmed to Recorded Future News that the vendor was Soapbox Engage — an online fundraising and engagement software platform for nonprofits.

Carnegie uses Soapbox Engage for online donations. The hackers stole names, email addresses, phone numbers and giving history.

“Due to an integration with Carnegie's database, the unauthorized users were able to temporarily gain access to contact information maintained by Carnegie,” Aly said. “Based on our investigation, a small percentage of our database was affected. No full credit card numbers were exposed.”

Aly added that the organization notified everyone in their database about the breach due to European data privacy laws. Carnegie hired a cybersecurity firm to confirm the hackers were removed from the system.

Aly did not say how many people were impacted by the incident. Founded in 1910, the think tank currently employs more than 200 experts. The institute supports research efforts, diplomacy and more.

In its last financial statement , the non-profit declared more than $522 million in assets and an operating revenue of about $51 million.

Soapbox Engage did not respond to requests for comment but the incident coincides with the disclosure of data breaches and cyberattacks by multiple digital donation platforms.

Fundraising software company DonorPerfect said it was impacted by a security incident last week, and on Friday GoFundMe said it identified a cybersecurity incident where hackers breached their systems in two separate attacks in October and November. GoFundMe reported the cyberattack to law enforcement.

“We have reviewed the data involved, and determined it included limited personal information such as names, postal addresses, phone numbers and, for some, dates of birth or other information provided voluntarily in registration forms,” the company said.

No card numbers or bank details were exposed and GoFundMe said they “addressed the known methods of unauthorized access, disabled unauthorized accounts, and are actively monitoring for further suspicious activity.

### CISA warns of exploited N-able bug as company restores email platform
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-24T15:00:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The U.S. cyber agency is giving government departments until Thanksgiving to patch a vulnerability impacting a critical email security platform that blocks spam, malware and other threats.
- Business background / implications: The U.S. cyber agency is giving government departments until Thanksgiving to patch a vulnerability impacting a critical email security platform that blocks spam, malware and other threats.

The federal cybersecurity agency is giving U.S. government departments until Thanksgiving to patch a vulnerability impacting a critical email security platform that blocks spam, malware and other threats.

All federal agencies were urged to patch CVE-2026-134624 – a bug affecting N-able’s Mail Assure platform. The vulnerability carries a 9.3 severity score and was added to the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency’s (CISA) exploited vulnerabilities platform on Monday evening.

N-able has dealt with a week of outages tied to the vulnerability after hackers breached one of the company’s servers through the bug, causing damage to Mail Assure and SpamExperts services.

In a statement to Recorded Future News, N-able said it remediated the bug over the weekend and restored access to internal dashboards for customers.

Inbound and outbound email flow and spam filtering continued to operate throughout the incident, they added.

“Since completing these remediation actions, we have continued scanning and monitoring the Mail Assure environments and have found no evidence of exploitation beyond the single N-able-hosted server identified in our initial investigation,” a spokesperson said, adding that they hired incident responders to validate their findings.

The company pledged to publish a root cause analysis within the next two weeks.

N-able said the breach and service issues are not connected to more than 30 vulnerabilities impacting its N-central platform that were publicicized two weeks ago by a security researcher.

N-central is a popular remote management tool that thousands of IT teams use to monitor and run networks remotely. N-able took all hosted N-central environments offline and ordered customers to turn off their servers.

A bug in the company’s N-central platform was involved in a supply-chain attack by ransomware actors in August .

N-able was previously a business unit within software company SolarWinds before it was spun off as a separate cloud-based software solutions company offering tools to managed service providers.

### Tomorrow’s quantum threat is today’s open door
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: CyberScoop | Published: 2026-11-24T11:00:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: Post-quantum cryptography deserves a place on the roadmap. But in operational technology, the threats doing damage today are far more basic, and far more urgent. The post Tomorrow’s quantum threat is today’s open door appeared first on CyberScoop .
- Business background / implications: OT leaders should prioritize network segmentation, asset inventory, and basic security controls before chasing post-quantum cryptography.

Earlier this month, attackers switched refrigeration systems in supermarkets across nine European countries into defrost mode . Stores were forced to throw out spoiled stock, and as is now being reported, some blood banks in France were hit as well. Around the same time, U.S. agencies including the FBI and CISA warned that internet-exposed refrigeration and temperature controllers were being actively targeted.

The attackers didn’t get in by breaking encryption or cracking passwords. They found admin interfaces exposed to the internet that never should have been public facing, along with known vulnerabilities and, in some cases, default credentials. In other words, unnecessary attack surfaces, unpatched systems, and factory default passwords. They didn’t need a quantum cryptography breakthrough. They had an open door.

That’s worth keeping in mind as the post-quantum cryptography (PQC) conversation picks up speed. New algorithms have been standardized , governments are setting migration timelines , and vendors are urging organizations to start migrating. Quantum computing’s potential to break widely used encryption deserves serious preparation. But nearly all of that guidance is targeted at IT. In operational technology (OT), the PQC timeline looks very different, and the risks hurting organizations today are frankly much lower-hanging fruit.

Before worrying about tomorrow’s lockpick technology, lock today’s open doors.

The devices that run water, electric, critical infrastructure, and manufacturing environments were built for a different era and a different purpose. Some examples are PLCs (programmable logic controllers), HMIs (human-machine interfaces) and OITs (operator interface terminals), as well as RTUs (remote terminal units) and PMRs (pole-mounted routers) in the electric sector.

Start with lifecycles. NIST’s “Guide to Operational Technology (OT) Security” (SP 800-82 Rev. 3) notes that OT technology often remains deployed for 10 to 15 years, and sometimes longer. In practice, it’s often much longer. Organizations don’t always inventory these devices, or even realize they’re on the network, so they can sit in public-sector environments for 20 years or more. I’m a millennial, and I’ve assessed equipment at an electrical site that was last updated before I was born.

Then there’s the hardware factor. OT devices

### Virginia, Oregon hit SpaceX with fresh lawsuits over Grok-created CSAM
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: CyberScoop | Published: 2026-11-23T21:32:33Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The lawsuit alleges that weak safeguards around nudification were a primary selling point for Musk’s business strategy to differentiate Grok and other competing AI models. The post Virginia, Oregon hit SpaceX with fresh lawsuits over Grok-created CSAM appeared first on CyberScoop .
- Business background / implications: Virginia and Oregon attorneys general sued SpaceX and Elon Musk, alleging Grok allowed users to create nonconsensual deepfake nudes.

Two new lawsuits accuse SpaceX and its subsidiaries of violating state consumer and trade laws by allowing its AI tool, Grok, to create deepfake nudes of millions of people earlier this year.

Virginia Attorney General Jay Jones and Oregon Attorney General Dan Rayfield, both Democrats, filed the lawsuits. They allege SpaceX and owner Elon Musk repeatedly failed to block Grok’s nudification feature after it became clear the tool was being used to break the law by creating nude or defamatory images of real people. The attorneys general want a court to bar SpaceX from advertising or profiting from the tool’s capabilities, pay restitution to victims and impose civil penalties.

“Time and again, xAI and X told consumers they were being protected from abuses. That was false,” Jones said in a statement. “Instead, the tool permitted users to routinely create and circulate non-consensual intimate imagery and sexualized images, and child sexual abuse material (CSAM), causing harm to individuals and children. Companies that deploy powerful tools don’t get to ignore consumer safety and call it innovation. The law applies to everyone, and this office will hold big tech accountable when they break the law.”

The lawsuits say Grok launched when other AI companies already knew about the harms of deepfake pornography to adults and children. Many of those companies built safeguards to keep their models from generating such content. Musk, by contrast, is facing separate lawsuits from former victims of child sexual abuse material. Those plaintiffs allege Grok can easily nudify children because it was partly trained on real abuse material found online.

“Led by founder Elon Musk, Defendants chose to ignore this growing industry concern, and instead saw a market opportunity for more profits,” the lawsuit alleged. “To push its lagging product, Grok, to tens of millions of new users, it stripped out industry-standard safeguards and marketed a ‘spicy mode’ built to produce nudity.”

As CyberScoop reported last year, Musk’s public statements and decisions to maintain Grok’s capabilities were so brazen that cybersecurity and privacy lawyers predicted he would face an avalanche of criminal and civil actions . Over the past six months, lawsuits, federal and state investigations, and international regulatory scrutiny have battered the company.

The lawsu

## Behavior-based detection engineering

Generic behavior hunts are maintained separately and are not attributed to a specific campaign without source evidence:
- [Suspicious PowerShell/process execution](../../../detection-queries/kql/threat-hunting/windows_suspicious_execution.kql)
- [Scheduled-task/service persistence](../../../detection-queries/kql/threat-hunting/windows_persistence_changes.kql)
- [Entra privileged role and credential changes](../../../detection-queries/kql/threat-hunting/entra_privilege_changes.kql)

## Collection errors

- None.

## Analyst workflow

1. Match KEV/advisories to asset inventory, versions and internet exposure.
2. Verify exploit status, affected versions and remediation against primary vendor/CERT advisories.
3. Derive behavior hunts from documented TTPs; map ATT&CK only when supported by evidence.
4. Verify candidate IOCs against original source, freshness and local telemetry.
5. Keep queries Draft until schema, syntax, positive/negative tests and tuning are recorded.

## Limitations

- Broad public-source baseline, not exhaustive global intelligence.
- ThreatFox, OTX, GreyNoise and Shadowserver are not enabled; review credentials and provider terms before integration.
- Business discovery uses a wider 7-day window; Google News results are reported leads and require company-announcement or filing verification.
- Feed failures and quiet periods do not mean absence of threats; report timestamps are IST and machine timestamps in JSON are UTC.

Raw evidence: [JSON snapshot](../../../snapshots/2026/11/25.json).
